## Berkeley Fire staffing, callback and mutual aid (public sources), 2026-11-01

Research for the staffing design note (`docs/design/fire-staffing.md`, HANDOFF item 7), asked for by Eric (his row of 2026-11-01 above). Public sources only, all retrieved 2026-11-01 with a generic User-Agent; raw downloads kept outside the repo. Nothing in the simulator changed.

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| FS1 | Normal in-service staffing: "Seven (7) engine companies with three (3) personnel each", "Two (2) truck companies with three (3) personnel each", "Four (4) ambulances with two (2) personnel each", and a Paramedic Supervisor I counted in minimum staffing (Memorandum of Agreement, City of Berkeley and Berkeley Fire Fighters Association, IAFF Local 1227, July 1, 2023 to June 30, 2026, §39.1, PDF p. 66; berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/Memorandum-of-Understading-City-Berkeley-Fire-Fighters-Association_0.pdf). As paramedics replace firefighters on ambulances, a fourth firefighter goes on one truck, then the second truck, then one engine, then a second engine pending a SAFER grant (§42.3–42.6, pp. 67–68). Companies at two are a "2-Person Medical Unit", normally out of service for fires (§40.2). The chief may change staffing for "additional coverage during critical fire weather" (§41.1). | **context**: refines G8. Our derived daily on-duty total is 37 (35 apparatus seats, the EMS supervisor, one battalion chief) to about 45 (six units at four per Citygate, plus Engines 4 and 7 on a fire-weather day); firefighters on fire companies 27 to 35. Still derived: no source states the total. |
| FS2 | Shift pattern: Berkeley Fire's base schedule is 56 hours a week (FY26 Mid-Biennial Fire presentation, 2025-05-22, slide 2); the MOU speaks of staff "per shift" in three or four platoons (§42.1–42.2). Item 49 (2024-05-07, p. 3) gives 120 firefighters on the nine companies. 12% of staff (21 FTE) were on injury leave or in vacant positions needing backfill (FY26 presentation, slide 3). | **context**: derived, about two-thirds of line staff are off duty at any moment; this is the pool a callback draws on. |
| FS3 | Recall rules: "Nothing prevents the immediate use of emergency recall based on a local or regional emergency that requires extra staffing as quickly as possible" (MOU §38.11, p. 65); emergency call-back pays travel time with a four-hour minimum (§16.11.2–16.11.3, p. 30). The MOU sets no response time and no residence distance. | **context**: no public recall time standard; any callback delay in the simulator is an assumption. The MOU expired June 30, 2026; a successor was not checked. |
| FS4 | Special Item 1c, "EMBER: Leave Early and Fire Department Staffing" (Council, 2025-02-11): Citygate's 2023 Standards of Cover asks for a fourth firefighter on six of nine units (Rec. 5), overtime upstaffing of Engines 3, 4 and 7, "closest to the eastern hills", in high-hazard periods (Rec. 6; Engine 3 permanent by mid-2025, Engines 4 and 7 at about $3,600 a day), a FEMA SAFER grant for a fourth firefighter on three engines, and a second battalion chief (Rec. 10) (pp. 4–5). If evacuation starts after ignition in extreme weather, "responders divert efforts from property conservation to supporting evacuation" (p. 1). In a major wildfire "numerous emergencies often occur simultaneously—the wildfire itself, structure fires ignited by embers, medical emergencies including burns and smoke inhalation, calls for help from trapped residents, and traffic collisions during evacuation" (p. 6). | **context**: the competing demands and the hill-station list in the design note. The SAFER count (three engines) differs from the MOU's §42.6 (a second engine); recorded, not resolved. |
| FS5 | Berkeley's 1991 recall: Engine 3 arrived at Tunnel and Vicente Roads at 12:08 p.m. and asked for a full first alarm (2 engines, 1 truck); second alarm 12:17, recall of chief officers and the "B" platoon for reserve apparatus (3 engines, 1 truck) at 12:22, reserves "staffed by 1:30 p.m.", third alarm 12:38; first mutual-aid requests to Albany (12:55), Emeryville and Lawrence Berkeley Laboratory (1:50 p.m.), one engine each (Cal OES, *The East Bay Hills Fire: A Multi-Agency Review of the October 1991 Fire in the Oakland/Berkeley Hills*, 1992, PDF pp. 25, 27; caloes.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/Fire-Rescue/Documents/OES-Multi-Agency-Review-East-Bay-Hills-Fire.pdf). Oakland's off-duty members "continued reporting for duty through 4:00 a.m., on October 21st" (p. 27). While all of Berkeley's companies were committed, one engine was diverted to a separate structure fire in Berkeley (USFA TR-060, PDF p. 41). | **context**: the only Berkeley callback time on record: reserves staffed within 68 minutes of the order (an upper bound; the record does not say when the first recalled crews were ready), on a Sunday with roads outside the fire open. The design note cites it as context for a callback range chosen as an assumption (Codex review on PR #17). |
| FS6 | 1991 fire and mutual aid (J. Gordon Routley, *The East Bay Hills Fire, Oakland-Berkeley, California*, USFA-TR-060, 1991; caloes.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/Fire-Rescue/Documents/US-Fire-Admin-East-Bay-Hills-Fire-Report.pdf): flare-up and full box alarm 10:58 a.m. (PDF p. 29); strike teams of five engines each; San Francisco's two teams requested 12:18–12:29, en route by 12:40 (p. 39); the first strike teams reached Hiller Highlands between 1:15 and 1:30 p.m., after every structure there had burned, and teams sent to stage at Hiller and Tunnel Roads were slowed by traffic on Highway 13 (pp. 43–44); "Almost 800 structures were ignited within the first hour and more than 300 per hour for the next seven hours" (p. 61); 440 engine companies and more than 1,500 firefighters in all; crews "had to retreat and evacuate residents ahead of fire" (summary table, p. 12). By late afternoon 370 engines were in or on their way to Oakland (Chief Reginald Garcia, California Fire Instructors Workshop paper, caloes.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/Fire-Rescue/Documents/CFIW-Oakland-Berkeley-Hills.pdf, p. 5); mutual-aid engines lost water when power to pumps failed. | **context**: first strike teams arrived about 2¼–2½ hours after the flare-up. The first-hour figure is why the design note expects callback timing to matter less than on-duty staffing for the simulator's results. |
| FS7 | Where Berkeley Fire members live: no public aggregate was found (searched City reports and Council items, the union's site and statements, Berkeleyside, The Berkeley Scanner, 2026-11-01). Comparison only: "71 percent of San Francisco firefighters live outside of San Francisco" (Mission Local, July 2019, from records it requested), and SFFD's spokesperson said "Recall is going to be very speculative". State law: no local agency may require its employees to live in the agency (Gov. Code §50083); a "reasonable and specific distance" may be required (Cal. Const. art. XI §10(b)); a 40-mile radius was upheld in *IAFF v. City of San Leandro* (1986) 181 Cal.App.3d 179. The Berkeley MOU has no distance rule (FS3). | **context**: Eric's premise (many members live outside Berkeley) stays his statement, unquantified. A callback delay can only be a labeled assumption. Never request or store individual data; distance bands in aggregate would be the most to use. |
| FS8 | Mutual aid system: Cal OES Operations Bulletin 1 (April 2022) lets an Operational Area order up to five strike teams or task forces of "immediate need" resources directly from one adjacent Operational Area. No statewide arrival-time standard was found. Santa Clara County's Operational Area plan (Appendix 2, rev. April 2021, sccfd.org) expects mutual aid within the Operational Area to "respond to the incident within 30 minutes from time of dispatch". Oakland's Wildland Fire Annex (Sept. 2021) names the Alameda County Fire Chief as the Operational Area's Fire and Rescue Mutual Aid Coordinator. The Alameda County Operational Area plan is still not online (G9). | **context**: the Santa Clara figure is another county's dispatch target, not an arrival time for Berkeley; not used as a number. The LHMP's warning that outside departments may not provide mutual aid after a Hayward earthquake (p. B-51, G9) bounds the regional-disaster case. |
| FS9 | 2025 Palisades Fire: "LAFD command staff determined not to hold over off going LAFD members to fill every available seat"; "A recall of all members to fill every available seat was implemented after the start of the Palisades incident" (LAFD After-Action Review, quoted by ABC7, 2025-10-08). LAFD now recalls all staff and staffs all available apparatus during every Red Flag Warning (LAFD, AARR Fact Sheet, lafd.org/sites/default/files/pdf_files/AARR_Fact_Sheet.pdf). | **context**: pre-event staffing (holdover, upstaffing on fire-weather days, Berkeley's Engines 4 and 7) shapes the first hours more than callback speed. |
| FS10 | Station 4 (1900 Marin Ave): proposed for closure in the FY 2027 budget, with nine firefighter positions; it stays open if a half-cent sales tax passes in November 2026, and its closure returns to the Council if it fails (The Berkeley Scanner, 2026-05-21; Bay City News via Local News Matters, 2026-06-27). Chief Sprague told the Council that Station 7 (Shasta Rd) is the slowest but isolated, which is why it was not the one proposed (Scanner, as reported). Engine 4 ran 1,538 calls in 2025 (City figure in the Scanner; also in the union president's op-ed, 2026-04-20). | **context**: a sourced "Station 4 closed" setting is part of option (a1) in the design note. Undecided as of 2026-11-01 (election 2026-11-03). |
| FS11 | Competing demands with figures: one house fire needs five to six apparatus and at least 15 firefighters (M4), so the 9 on-duty fire companies (FS1) are fewer than the 10 to 12 apparatus two such fires need: they can fully resource one house fire at a time. Counting firefighters alone (27 to 35 against 15 per fire) gives one to two fires, a personnel-only equivalent, not fire coverage (derived; corrected after Codex review on PR #17). The Time Study ranks staffed traffic control points "among the numerous competing life-safety needs" (p. 11-1) and wants signals that "minimize the need for emergency personnel to manually direct traffic" (p. 11-7). Assisted evacuation: E7 and E29 (1,686 people without a car or ride; Panoramic Hill alone needs 13 buses, 28 wheelchair vans and 27 ambulances) against Berkeley's 4 ambulances (FS1). | **context**: options (b) and (c) of the design note. |
| FS12 | Scratch test (not in the simulator; a script reusing `engineReachStats`): east-wind fire, central, night (S6), seeds 1–5, hill residents in Berkeley zones (about 27,850) beyond 5 minutes at ignition with stations removed from the source list. Medians, status quo / one side only: all stations 1,510 / 1,010; without Station 3 1,510 / 1,010; without 7 2,569 / 1,514; without 4 2,865 / 1,334; without 7 and 4 5,892 (4,863–6,176) / 3,311 (2,548–3,448); without 7, 4, 3 and 2 9,326 / 8,326; only 1 and 6 left 11,587 / 10,392. No-route counts do not change, so the increase is homes beyond 5 minutes from the remaining stations. | **context**: model results on synthetic households, not a prediction; same engine-speed limits as ER3, and no move-up coverage. The basis for recommending option (a1) first. |
| FS13 | **No City containment estimate (searched 2026-11-02, Eric: "does the city say how long it would take them to get the fire under control?").** Searched berkeleyca.gov, the Community Wildfire Protection Plan (April 2023, berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/Community-Wildfire-Protection-Plan_CWPP.pdf), Fire Department supplements and Council items, and press coverage: none gives a time to contain or control a hills fire. The 2025 Evacuation Time Study models no fire (E31); the StoryMap gives spread timing only (S10–S11). The CWPP's fire history says the 1923 fire "started in Wildcat Canyon and spread west to Hearst Avenue and Shattuck Avenue" (584 structures), with no control time. | **context**: the simulator's fire is never controlled; a run's end (4 h, 6 h for the 1923 repeat) is not a containment time. |
| FS14 | **What the Fire Department says instead** (Supplemental memo, Council 2024-07-23 Item 01, Middle Housing, Fire Department, dev.berkeleyca.gov/sites/default/files/documents/2024-07-23%20Special%20330%20Item%2001%20Zoning%20Ordinance%20and%20General%20-%20Fire%20Dept%20Supp%201.pdf): "When a fire builds to this magnitude and travels with extreme speed and force, firefighters' primary focus often shifts from firefighting to supporting evacuations" (p. 3); aircraft "need to be grounded during high winds" (p. 3); "residents in Berkeley's WUI may not be able to evacuate as fast as a fire could spread" (p. 4); "evacuation must occur before a fire starts" (p. 4); "The Berkeley Fire Department currently lacks adequate staffing to respond effectively to severe wildland urban interface fires entering the city" (p. 5). | **context**: supports the simulator's choice that engines do not fight the fire, and the weight Change the plan puts on leaving early. |
| FS15 | **1991 containment** (USFA-TR-060, as FS6): "As long as the wind was present, the fire was going to continue to spread, no matter what strategy and tactics were used … The fire was contained only when the wind changed" (PDF p. 55); "The fire was declared as contained on Tuesday morning" (PDF p. 54), about two days after the Sunday 10:58 a.m. flare-up; "Just 64 minutes after the fire broke out, it was burning in the Rockridge district, more than a mile downwind" (p. 55). The 1923 fire likewise stopped spreading when moist westerlies replaced the Diablo wind, about 2.5 h after it entered the built area (S10). | **context**: in both historical fires the wind, not firefighting, ended the spread. |

## Contraflow, police control of signals and a Red Flag power shutoff (public sources and simulator results), 2026-11-01

Built at Eric's request (his rows of 2026-11-01 above): three Expert-view settings, all off by default, in `js/operations.js`. Public sources retrieved 2026-11-01 with a generic User-Agent; downloads kept outside the repository. Figures marked "not verified" came from a search excerpt because the page itself would not load.

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| CF1 | City `Signals` layer (G4), fetched again 2026-11-01 with point geometry and `ControlTyp` only (`node tools/fetch-city-layers.mjs signals`): 146 signals, 102 "Actuated Signal" and 44 "Pretimed Signal"; layer last edited 2025-06-06. `node tools/signals.mjs` snaps each to the nearest junction of 3 or more drivable streets within 40 m: 145 snap, onto 143 junctions (two junctions carry two points each), 1 does not; the 44 pre-timed signals land on 44 junctions. 30 signal junctions touch a Fire Zones 2–3 street or lie within 150 m of one (model rule for the "hills" shutoff). | **applied**: `data/model/signals.json` (derived), read by the police-control and power-shutoff settings and shown in the Inspector at each end of a street. Rerun the tool after every network rebuild. |
| CF2 | Time Study §4.3 (p. 4-7): "At specific locations where it is advisable or where existing plans call for overriding existing traffic control to implement manned control, the model will use actuated signal timings that reflect the presence of traffic guides. At locations where a special traffic control strategy (continuous left-turns, contra-flow lanes) is used, the strategy is modeled explicitly." The study reports no contraflow and names no manned locations in its results; Rec. 1.3 asks the City to "prioritize specific locations for Traffic Control Points" from the Appendix F congestion diagrams (p. 11-1). | **applied**: the study's evacuation times assume neither setting, so calibration stays with both off (calibration note below). Appendix F junctions named in the text (pp. F-3 to F-5): Cedar St / Martin Luther King Jr Wy, Gayley Rd / Hearst Av, the Piedmont Av / Channing Wy circle, Piedmont Av / Dwight Wy, Derby St / Claremont (County name Claremont Bl), Ashby Av / Claremont Av; all six found in the network and added to `signals.json` as `appF`. |
| CF3 | Contraflow in a wildfire: NIST Technical Note 2252 (Camp Fire, 2018, July 2023). A 2016 drill ran Skyway one-way southbound "between 07:00 and 08:00" (p. 38); on the day Clark Rd was "fully implemented … for four lanes southbound by 09:00" (p. 92); "both lanes are contraflow westbound" on Bille Rd, a two-lane local road, from Pentz Rd to Skyway at 08:30 (p. 101). Paradise's contraflow plan did not provide for responders driving into town against the evacuation (p. 30). | **applied**: two-lane streets can run both lanes out (Eric's idea has precedent); the responder cost is modeled: engines cannot drive up a contraflow street. Start-up times stay assumptions (`operations.contraflow`). |
| CF4 | Capacity of a reversed lane: on I-55 in the Hurricane Katrina evacuation the contraflow lanes peaked at 1,842 vehicles per hour, "about 30 percent lower than the adjacent normal-flow lanes" (Wolshon, "Empirical Characterization of Mass Evacuation Traffic Flow", TRB 2008 preprint 08-0930, pp. 9–10). The same paper cites the HCM's 1,700 passenger cars per hour per direction on two-lane roads (p. 13), the figure the Time Study uses (§4, p. 4-5). | **applied**: `operations.contraflow.reversedLaneFactor` 0.7, an assumption from freeway data; no two-lane-street figure was found. |
| CF5 | MUTCD 11th Edition (December 2023), Part 4: lane-use control signals are now **Chapter 4T** (Chapter 4M in the 2009 edition, 4J in 2003; 4M is now emergency-vehicle signals). §4T.01: an engineering study should decide between signs and signals, and signals are recommended where more than one lane is reversed; §4T.03: each reversed lane gets a green arrow and a red X, faces "clearly visible for at least 2,300 feet", more faces on curves so drivers see at least one; §4T.04: no opposing green arrows, the red X lasts long enough for the lane to clear, any automatic system has a manual override, and signals for infrequent use may stay dark when not in use (pp. 754–756). | **conflict** with the brief, which cited Chapter 4M: the current edition's chapter is 4T. **Applied**: the red-X clearing time is the reason for the 10-minute sign delay. Not modeled: on a winding hill street the MUTCD's visibility rule needs signal faces along the street, not only at the entry points, so the cost per entry point understates a compliant installation. |
| CF6 | Unit costs. LED blank-out "NO RIGHT TURN" sign, furnished and installed: $6,985 (City of La Vista, NE, council agenda item, 2024-04-16; one quote). Single-faced lane-use control signal: $32,000–$50,000 (2019, USDOT ITS cost database; not verified, the database refused the request). Lane control gates: $100,000–$150,000 per location in 1995 dollars (same database; not verified). Traffic signal battery backup: "a complete new system is approximately $7,000" (City of Eastvale, CA, FY2023-24 capital plan); about $5,000 per intersection in 2001 (SB 84XX Senate floor analysis, 2001-09-10). | **applied**: `operations.contraflow.costPerEntryUSD` (assumption): grid $7k–$50k, battery $14k–$57k per entry point; gates not costed; no figure for mechanical fold-out signs. Shown in the plan note and the event log. |
| CF7 | Berkeley Police staffing: 154 sworn filled, 144 available, 174 authorized in 2025 (BPD 2025 Annual Report slides, slide 4); 27 officer vacancies and heavy overtime "to meet minimum staffing" (FY25-26 budget presentation, slides 6–7); patrol minimum staffing of 60 sworn officers in 7 teams of 4 to 9 with two sergeants each (City memo "BPD Patrol Unit Minimum Staffing", 2022-05-05); a 2026 patrol schedule of "just 54 officers, about 7-8 per team" (Berkeley Scanner, 2026-04-14, quoting police). UC Berkeley Police: 49 sworn officers in August 2025 (UCPD web page). | **applied**: the officer pool options (10, 20, 40, 80; default 20) are labeled an assumption with these figures as scale: one or two patrol teams are on duty at a time, so 40 or 80 means callback or mutual aid. **Conflict**: Berkeley Scanner gives 181 authorized and about 137 sworn (the union's figure), against the City's 174 and 154; the City's figures are used. Officers per junction and per contraflow entry point: 1 (assumption; no public staffing ratio for contraflow was found). |
| CF8 | Manual control at junctions. Mahalel, Gur and Shiftan (1991): manual control "increased intersection capacity by as much as 9 percent" at oversaturated isolated junctions (cited in Parr and Wolshon, SWUTC/15/600451-00113-1, 2015, p. 30). The same report measured about equal throughput with an officer and with an actuated signal, and average delay at Nicholson Dr / Roosevelt rose from 11.7 s to 42.1 s with the officer (pp. 84–88). A simulation of evacuations found manual control gave "no significant benefit" in rural or less congested areas and that "in densely populated regions clearance times increased significantly" (Parr, Herrera, Wolshon and Smith, TRR 2674(9), 2020). FHWA: signals "may be operated manually by a law enforcement officer" (FHWA-HOP-06-109). The Time Study wants signals that "minimize the need for emergency personnel to manually direct traffic" (§11.3.2, p. 11-7). | **applied**: `operations.police.capacityUplift` 0.09 (assumption), the high end of the published range, plus priority for approaches heading toward safety. Read police-control results as an optimistic case. |
| CF9 | All-way stops. HCM base saturation headways at a single-lane all-way stop: 3.9 s with no conflicting vehicles, up to 9.6 s (Exhibit 21-15, reproduced in the NCHRP 08-135 Rural Highway Guidebook, Ch. 10); 3,600 ÷ 3.9 ≈ 920 vehicles per hour. Signalized base saturation flow 1,900 per lane (FHWA HPMS Field Manual, App. N). The Time Study gives traffic circles, "often functioning as all-way stops", about 900 vehicles per hour per lane (App. G, p. G-1; E16). California Vehicle Code §21800(d)(1): at an inoperative signal a driver "shall stop at the intersection, and may proceed with caution when it is safe to do so". | **applied**: a dark signal passes at most `operations.powerShutoff.allWayStopVph` 900 vehicles per hour (assumption), against the model's 1,200 (collector) or 1,600 (arterial). |
| CF10 | PG&E Public Safety Power Shutoff, 26–28 October 2019: Berkeley circuits Arlington 0401, Ridge 0401/0402, Substation F 0402/1103/1105, Substation G 1105 and Substation K 1101/1102, all wholly or partly in Tier 2 or 3 fire-threat areas, were shut off between 20:14 on 10/26 and 00:11 on 10/27 and restored between 11:01 and 18:31 on 10/28; the City Manager and the Police and Fire Chiefs were told on 10/24 (PG&E post-event report to the CPUC, amended, appendix pp. 77–91). An earlier shutoff on 9–10 October 2019 cut power to about 3,500 Berkeley Hills customers, and the Mayor advised treating unpowered signals as stop signs (Mayor's blog, now offline; not verified). CPUC minimum notice: 48–72 hours to public safety partners, 24–48 hours to other customers (CPUC citation to PG&E, October 2023 event, p. 2). No source says which Berkeley signals went dark. | **applied**: Scenario setting "Red Flag power shutoff": none, signals in and next to the hills (30, CF1), or all. Shutoffs last longer than a run and start a day or more ahead, so battery backup may already be drained (CF11). |
| CF11 | Battery backup. Caltrans District 11 (2025-01-07): signals on battery "function normally for approximately three hours", then flash red for "an additional three to six hours", then go dark. The Time Study recommends upgrading "all traffic signal cabinets to include battery backup systems that can operate for a minimum of 6-8 hours" (Rec. 8.2). No public count of Berkeley signals with backup was found. | **applied**: no signal is assumed to have backup; contraflow signs with "battery backup" are assumed to last the run, which a shutoff that began 30 or more hours before the fire would not allow (disclosed in Assumptions & limits). |
| CF12 | Main hill exit streets (model rule, `node tools/contraflow-streets.mjs`, about 30 s on 8 cores): status-quo runs, fire on, central severity, seeds 1–3 on the three Simple-view fires; a two-way street segment with one lane each way, in Fire Zones 2–3 (or the 90 m proxy beyond the city line) or ending at a hill junction, qualifies when at least 300 order-zone vehicles drove it in the median run of one fire, 90% or more of them the same way (that way is "outbound"); plus the entrances of access-constrained areas with 100 or more residents. Result: 174 segments on 70 streets, 21.9 km; 13 segments come from choke entrances. Longest: Centennial Dr, Lawrence Rd, Spruce St, Euclid Av, Grizzly Peak Blvd, The Alameda, Shattuck Av, Santa Barbara Rd, Gayley Rd, Shasta Rd. Of the hill streets the Time Study found congested (App. F), Spruce St, Grizzly Peak Blvd, Euclid Av, The Alameda and Gayley Rd are in it; Marin Av, Arlington Av, Woodmont Av, Boynton Av, La Loma Av, Tunnel Rd and Claremont Av fell below the 300-vehicle bar in these runs. | **applied**: `data/model/contraflow-streets.json`, the "main hill exit streets" option. Rerun after every network rebuild. A street runs contraflow only while its zone is ordered or warned, so each fire uses the part of the list in its order area. **Corrected after Codex review on PR #19:** `js/operations.js` took the earliest order or warning across a whole connected piece of contraflow streets, so segments in zones the run never orders or warns started with the rest (east wind, central, seed 1, battery signs: 11 segments at t+20 min, among them Eunice St and Vine St in BER-027 and The Alameda in BER-026), closing their inbound direction and holding back engines. Segments in zones the run never orders or warns now stay two-way, and pieces form only among segments whose zones are told at the same time, so a segment never starts before its zone is ordered or warned; entry points and officers are counted on those pieces. Where pieces told at different times meet, the junction counts as an entry point of the earlier piece. The sign cost in the event log still covers every entry point on the wide-enough segments, since signs are installed whichever fire comes. Robustness rerun in CF14. |
| CF13 | **Where contraflow can run** (scratch script over `parkingState`, the 174 main-exit segments, seeds 1–5, day S4 and night S6, so 10 parking draws). Two outbound lanes need 20 ft clear between parked cars. Of 21.9 km, the length that qualifies on average: status quo 9.1 km (41%); one-side parking in Fire Zones 2–3 11.4 km (52%); no parking in Fire Zones 2–3 16.4 km (75%). Always or nearly always: The Alameda, Belrose Av, Hilgard Av, Hopkins St, Derby St, Piedmont Ci, Claremont Bl (91%), Channing Wy (81%), Vine St (78%), Centennial Dr (75%). Where a red curb (no parking) would add an evacuation benefit, because the pavement is 20 ft or wider and only parked cars block it (110 segments, 13.7 km): Spruce St (56% of draws → 100% with one-side parking), Grizzly Peak Blvd (26% → 80%, 94% with none), Euclid Av (51% → 82%), Warring St (74% → 100%), and narrower 22–26 ft streets that need no parking at all: Santa Barbara Rd, Regal Rd, Park Hills Rd, Keeler Av, San Luis Rd, Shasta Rd (each 2–69% → 100%). Where only widening would help (pavement under 20 ft; 8 segments, 885 m): Panoramic Wy (15–17 ft), San Diego Rd (19 ft), Sunset Ln (17 ft) and one 18 ft block of Euclid Av. Width unknown, so never eligible: Gayley Rd, Stadium Rim Rd, Sports Ln, Lee Rd and other UC and Lab roads. Flats streets on the list, such as Bonita Av and Virginia St, keep their parking under every hill parking option. | **context**: the Inspector says for each segment whether contraflow runs and, where it gains nothing, the clear width it falls short with. No `docs/widening.md` exists on main, so these numbers are the first list of where widening or a red curb would add an evacuation benefit; a widening study should start from it. |
| CF14 | **Robustness, 270 runs** (scratch runner; the three Simple-view fires at their default times of day: east wind at night S6, 1923 repeat and Panoramic Hill on a fall weekday S4; low, central and high severity × seeds 1–5; each variant against the status quo on the same seed; every run had the same households and departures as its status-quo pair). Rerun 2026-11-01 after the zone-eligibility correction in CF12 (Codex review on PR #19), on the network with The Crossways open and BER-013's Census count (K8, Z2), so status-quo figures differ from the first run, which used the earlier network. Variants: (i) contraflow on the main hill exit streets, pre-installed signs with battery backup; (ii) the same with grid-only signs, no fallback, and a power shutoff of the hill signals; (iii) officer-staffed contraflow, 20 officers arriving 30 min after the first order; (iv) police control of the 44 pre-timed signals, same pool and delay; (v) the hill power shutoff alone. Central medians, status quo → variant. **Effects that hold in all 15 same-seed pairs, all costs:** (i) lowers live engine reach (hill residents within 5 min, mean since the first order) on the east-wind fire (90.2% → 90.0%) and the 1923 repeat (83.1% → 81.0%), turns back more returning commuters on the 1923 repeat (3 → 25), and leaves more vehicles cut off on Panoramic Hill (263 → 283); (iii) lowers live engine reach on the 1923 repeat (83.1% → 82.3%). Close to every pair: (iii) turned back more commuters on the 1923 repeat in 14 of 15 (3 → 52) and left more vehicles cut off on Panoramic Hill in 10 of 15, fewer in 3 (263 → 282); (ii) lowered live engine reach on the 1923 repeat in 14 of 15 (83.1% → 83.0%). **No benefit holds in every pair for any variant on any fire.** The nearest: on the 1923 repeat, 90% clearance fell from 97.4 to 96.7 min with (iii) in 14 of 15 pairs (equal in 1) and to 96.8 min with (i) in 13 (higher in 1). People still there when embers or the fire front arrived move by 5% or less at the central median and in both directions across pairs (Panoramic Hill reaches 90% in only 5 of 15 runs). (ii): signs fail on all but 1–3 segments, so it behaves like (v). (iv): officers reach 9 junctions on the east-wind fire, 18 on the 1923 repeat and 3 on Panoramic Hill, with no consistent effect. (v): no consistent effect. Likely reasons (not tested): on most of these streets the junction at the bottom, whose capacity contraflow leaves unchanged, sets the flow; and the model's junction capacities by road class are already low (900–1,600 vehicles per hour), so a 9% uplift or a drop to 900 changes little. The Panoramic Hill cost: before the correction it came from Centennial Dr segments in Claremont Canyon running contraflow toward the fire although this fire never orders or warns their zones; those now stay two-way. What remains comes from contraflow segments near the fire: in seed 3, central (281 → 322 cut off), 20 vehicles end the run with no route on Piedmont Ci (BER-055) and 15 on Warring St (BER-057), both running contraflow, where the closed inbound direction leaves no way to turn back (inferred from where they stop; not traced vehicle by vehicle). | **applied**: no Simple-view question (none passes the P19 rule; a benefit question would need a measure that improves in every pair). The Assumptions & limits text says what each setting assumes; HANDOFF 7a lists what is open. Officer control and contraflow act on capacities the simulator does not tie to real signal phases (calibration gap, HANDOFF item 2), so a real effect could be larger or smaller. |
| CF15 | Not modeled: drivers who ignore the signs (an optional share was proposed; not built, since a wrong-way driver needs a crash and blockage model the simulator lacks); clearing inbound traffic off a street before it reverses beyond the start delay; rehearsal making people leave sooner; officers closing streets or directing turns; streetlights, phones, alerts and water pressure during a shutoff; battery drain. Officers do not go to contraflow and junctions the fire reaches before they arrive. | **context**: listed in Assumptions & limits. |
| CF16 | **Officer control at junctions with unlimited model capacity (Codex review, PR #19).** Before: a junction whose model capacity is unlimited (e.g. Shattuck Av / University Av, node 992) got an officer but no officer control, so in the 1923 fire, central, seed 1, with a citywide power shutoff, 18 junctions were reported controlled and 17 were. Now such a junction keeps unlimited capacity (a dark signal there gets it back) and gets outbound priority; 18 of 18. Police-only rerun (45 runs, same design as CF14): no measure moves the same way in every pair on any fire; central medians, people still there when embers arrived eastWind 5,083 → 5,079 (10 of 15 lower), ref1923 6,072 → 6,026 (10 of 15), panoramic 16,066 → 16,054 (7 of 15); 90% clearance ref1923 97.4 → 97.5 (11 of 15 lower). Junctions controlled 9, 18 and 3. | **applied**: police control's CF14 conclusion (no effect that holds in every pair) stands. |

## Ignition points and wind directions for fire families (public sources), 2026-11-02

Question (Eric, 2026-11-02): are the four fires' single ignition points and wind directions (`js/scenarios.js`: `ref1923`, `northHills` and `panoramic` from 45°, `eastWind` from 75°, all Diablo winds) the most probable, and why does no fire use a summer westerly? Research only, toward an optional "family" of ignition points × wind directions per fire; nothing in the simulator changed. All sources retrieved 2026-11-02 with a generic User-Agent; downloads kept outside the repository. "Our reading" marks what we took from a map image rather than from text.

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| FF1 | **XyloPlan, "Risk Assessment, Alameda County", 2025-02-10** (26-slide presentation; static1.squarespace.com/static/676593ab64e2656b9076c88a/t/67b63489335d2c550d89454c/1739994307064/Alameda+County+Fire+Pathway+Presentation_20250210.pdf, linked from Berkeley Fire's bulletin of 2025-02-19, content.govdelivery.com/accounts/CABERKE/bulletins/3d27c28). Method: Fire Pathways are "the routes formed by the combination of topography, weather, and continuous vegetative fuel that bring wildfire to values-at-risk" in "a fast-moving, wind-driven fire event" (p. 2). Fires are "Modeled with extreme, fast-moving wildfire events, without estimating ignition point probabilities" (p. 3); ignition lines are placed "at regular intervals" across the landscape (p. 4); FlamMap minimum travel time on a 5 m fuel model (Tukman Geospatial 2022) (pp. 4–5). Two weather scenarios per ignition line, "North / East and South / West", each from an actual RAWS record with cured fuels, sustained high wind and low humidity (pp. 4, 6–7): Oakland North RAWS, 2013-11-22 2:00, 35 mph; Oakland South RAWS, 2000-10-22 13:00, 44 mph (p. 7). The slides do not say which record drives which scenario and give no wind direction in degrees. | **pending: fire families**. XyloPlan places ignitions everywhere and asks where fire arrives; it supports choosing ignition points by entry corridor, not by ignition likelihood. |
| FF2 | **XyloPlan entry points for Berkeley (North/East scenario).** "While many ignition lines are used to draw Fire Pathways, the resulting pathways tend to converge on the same fuel corridors, the fastest routes fire will take" (p. 13). Entry points are "where high concentrations of pathways intersect with densely-spaced structures" (p. 16). Named focus areas: "Lake Anza" (pp. 17–19) and "Strawberry Canyon" (pp. 20–23). Our reading of the maps (pp. 15–16, 18, 20): a chain of community entry points along the Grizzly Peak Blvd edge from the Kensington line south past Lake Anza, fed by pathways from Tilden, with the highest-rated blocks just north of Tilden Golf Course, which interrupts the pathways ("Non-burnable areas interrupt Fire Pathways", p. 15); farther south, an entry point at the Panoramic Hill edge south-east of the stadium, fed by pathways from the east across the Strawberry Canyon hills, and a larger one about 1.5 km south-east of it in the Claremont Canyon area, fed by pathways from the Grizzly Peak ridge. Between the two groups (Cragmont south to the Lab) the maps show fewer pathways reaching homes. South/West scenario: it "has more opportunity to form pathways in the south and east of Alameda, while the north has relatively few pathways" (p. 11). | **pending: fire families**: the clearest public basis for where a north-east-wind fire enters Berkeley. The map reading is ours; the slides name no streets. |
| FF3 | **XyloPlan in Council records: not found.** The bulletin says "The City of Berkeley has already presented this analysis in PDF form to the Berkeley City Council and used it in several grant applications" (2025-02-19). Searched the 2025-02-11 special meeting annotated agenda (Items 1a–1d and their supplemental lists), Item 1c (Leave Early and Fire Department Staffing, 10 pp.), the EMBER fire code and implementation reports already read (M1–M5), the Disaster and Fire Safety Commission packets of 2025-02-26, 2025-05-28 and 2025-10-29, and a berkeleyca.gov site search: none mentions XyloPlan or fire pathways. The Fire Department's presentation listed as Supplemental Communications 3, item 11, on 2025-02-11 was not found online. | **context**: we cite the XyloPlan slides directly. If Berkeley Fire shares the Berkeley-specific PDF informally, it may name corridors; not needed for the families. |
| FF4 | **Diablo wind direction at the Oakland North RAWS (derived).** Oakland North RAWS (COKN; 37.865 N, 122.2208 W, 1,403 ft, "near Grizzly Peak and Marlborough Terrace in Oakland, above Gwin Canyon", EBRPD WHRRMP App. C, PDF p. 20), the station XyloPlan and EBRPD used. WRCC frequency tool (raws.dri.edu/cgi-bin/wea_freq.pl?caCOKN), hourly mean wind direction, June 1992 – October 2026, queried in 5-year blocks, hours with mean wind ≥ 15 mph and relative humidity ≤ 25%: 2,468 hours (about 0.8% of about 302,000 possible). Direction (from): 0–70° 86.9%; 20–70° 71.8%; 40–60° 36.1%; median in the 40–50° bin; 70–90° 1.9%; 340–360° 2.5%; south to south-west (150–230°) 6.3%. All hours of 2017 for comparison: the most common direction is 180–200° (29%). | **applied 2026-11-02**: `eastWind` and `eastWindDay` moved from 75° to 60° (P32, change log); the families are still pending. 45° is the central dry-wind direction at this station; 75° lay in a bin holding 1.5% of dry windy hours. One ridge-top station; terrain steers its wind, and directions over Berkeley's canyons will differ (Oakland VMP p. 15 table: in narrow canyons "Surface winds follow canyon direction"). |
| FF5 | **Diablo wind speed at the Oakland North RAWS (derived).** Same tool, hours with wind from 0–90° and relative humidity ≤ 25%, all months, June 1992 – October 2026: 7,340 hours; hourly mean wind ≥ 15 mph in 29.7%, ≥ 20 mph in 13.1%, ≥ 25 mph in 4.6%, ≥ 30 mph in 1.3% (98 hours); highest bin 60–65 mph (1 hour). Peak gust in the same filter (7,237 hours with a gust value): ≥ 30 mph in 21.1%, ≥ 40 mph in 7.4%, ≥ 50 mph in 2.1%, highest bin 70–80 mph (2 hours). RAWS wind is a 10-minute mean at 20 ft. Published descriptions run higher: "wind velocities of 35 to 70 miles per hour" for Diablo winds in general (USFA-TR-060, PDF p. 16); 1923, 25–40 mph (StoryMap §3.2, S10); 1991, "Wind gusts of 25 mph were commonplace in Oakland Hills on October 20, with some reports of wind gusts as high as 38–58 mph" (Weatherwise, Sept/Oct 2015, p. 10, ggweather.com/firestorm/Retrospect_2015_Oakland_Hills_Fire.pdf; magazine). | **pending: fire families**: the simulator's 30–35 mph sits in the top 0.3–1.3% of dry offshore hours at this station, consistent with a fire-scenario choice of an extreme event. |
| FF6 | **How much direction varies within an event.** EBRPD's fire modeling used the Oakland North hourly record for 2003-10-23 to 10-28, chosen because it was "among the windiest in the previous 10 years" and "consistent with a long-term Diablo Wind event" (Wildfire Hazard Reduction and Resource Management Plan, 2010, Appendix C, PDF p. 20; table PDF p. 25; ebparks.org/sites/default/files/WHRRMP-Appendix_C_-_Hazard_Assess_and_Trtmt_Areas.pdf). Our summary of that table (144 hours, speeds to 30 mph): hours ≥ 15 mph (67) blew from 8–74°, median 58°, 10th–90th percentile 31–70°; hours ≥ 25 mph (14), 41–67°, median 57°. The wind turned from about 10° on the evening of the 23rd to 50–70° from the 24th; lulls between surges swung through west. The two historic fires ended with a reversal to onshore wind: 1923, "moist westerlies" about 2.5 h after entry (S10); 1991, "a cool damp ocean breeze began to push the products of combustion back into the burn area" after the wind abated about 19:30 (USFA-TR-060, PDF p. 52). | **pending: fire families**: within one strong event the direction holds to roughly ±15–20° of its median; a family spanning about 30°–60° covers the core. A late shift to westerly is a separate effect the simulator does not model (FS15). |
| FF7 | **Diablo wind direction as the sources describe it.** City CWPP (April 2023, p. 11): "These winds are known locally as 'Diablo Winds' since they come from the north or northeast in the direction of Mount Diablo"; "Both the 1923 and 1991 fires burned with strong northeasterly (Diablo) winds" (p. 14). USFA-TR-060 (PDF p. 16): "a wind flow from northeast to southwest"; foehn winds "occur most frequently between mid-September and late November". Oakland Draft Vegetation Management Plan (May 2018, p. 12): "dry easterly (or northeasterly) winds". Smith, Hatchett and Kaplan (2018, *Fire* 1(2):25, mdpi.com/2571-6255/1/2/25) define a Diablo-like hour at a station as wind above 11.17 m/s (25 mph) from 315°–135° with relative humidity below 30%, from RAWS 1999–2018, in Wine Country and the western Sierra Nevada, not the East Bay; the paper page returned HTTP 403, so these criteria come from a search excerpt of the preprint and are **not verified**. The AMS abstract by the same group calls Diablo winds "dry and gusty north-northeasterly windstorms" (ams.confex.com/ams/18Mountain/webprogram/Paper345515.html). No NWS Bay Area Red Flag climatology with directions was found. | **context**: consistent with FF4; the station data narrow "north to east" to a core of about 20–70°. |
| FF8 | **Major fires with wind and ignition** (Hills Emergency Forum list, "Fires in the Oakland - Berkeley Hills", rev. 02/28/05, 2 pp., hosted by Cal OES: caloes.ca.gov/wp-content/uploads/Fire-Rescue/Documents/OES-Fires-in-the-Oakland-Berkeley-Hills-History.pdf). "The area's recorded fire history shows 15 major fires since the first fire documented in 1923"; "the fires often reoccur in the same general areas and show similar environmental conditions" (p. 1). Wind: Diablo 8 (1923, 1931, 1933, 1946, 1960, 1970, 1980, 1991); West 6 (1929, 1937, 1940, 1955, 1968, 1990); south-west 1 (1961, four arson fires in Tilden, Briones, Roberts and Chabot, 400 acres). Homes lost: Diablo fires 584 (1923), 5, 5, 0, 2, 37, 5 and 3,354 (1991); west-wind fires 0 except 1937 Broadway Terrace (4 homes, 700 acres). Ignitions given: smoker (1923, 1933), arson (1946 with rekindle, 1961, 1970), backyard fire (1937), power line (1980), vehicle accident (1990), rekindle (1991), unknown (the other five). The Oakland Draft VMP (May 2018, Table 6, pp. 68–69) repeats the list without 1929 and adds 2017 fires; it concludes "the largest and most damaging fires have occurred during such winds" (Diablo, p. 69). | **pending: fire families**: Diablo fires are the ones that destroyed homes, so the families stay Diablo-led; westerly fires are common (6 of 15) but have burned few homes. |
| FF9 | **Where the fires that reached Berkeley started.** 1923: "The fire started in Wildcat Canyon and spread west to Hearst Avenue and Shattuck Avenue" (CWPP p. 14); "approximately 2-1/2 miles north of Temescal Canyon" (USFA-TR-060, PDF p. 22). 1970: "on the eastern slope of the hills near Fish Ranch Road, just over the ridge from Temescal Canyon. It spread rapidly up the slope and jumped over Grizzly Peak Boulevard on a front 300 to 400 feet wide" and then into Temescal Canyon and "the upper parts of Grandview and Claremont Canyons" (USFA-TR-060, PDF p. 22). 1980: "also originated in Wildcat Canyon" (PDF p. 22), Wildcat Canyon Road (Oakland VMP Table 6). 1991: "on the steep slope at the very end of Temescal Canyon" (USFA-TR-060, PDF p. 25), "the west slope in the area where the 1970 fire began to spread on that side of the hills" (PDF p. 22, note 2). **Conflict on causes:** the Oakland Draft VMP says mowers ignited "the 1991 Tunnel Fire and the 1970 Diablo Fire" (p. 69); the HEF list gives arson (1970) and rekindle (1991); USFA says the 1991 fire's original cause "has not been determined" (PDF p. 21). | **pending: fire families**: the record supports two origin areas: Wildcat Canyon / Tilden east of the north hills (1923, 1980), and the ridge at Fish Ranch Rd / upper Temescal Canyon south of Claremont Canyon (1970, 1991). The cause conflict does not affect locations. |
| FF10 | **Ignition sources and locations named by agencies.** Oakland Draft VMP (May 2018, p. 69): ignitions are "directly related to human activity", notably "view spots along Grizzly Peak Boulevard or Skyline Boulevard", stolen-vehicle dump sites, mowers and power equipment, fireworks around July 4, and "vehicle-originated fires along Plan Area roads, including State Routes 13 and 24 and Interstate 580". USFA-TR-060 (PDF p. 21): "The Diablo winds often cause power lines to arc or short out, sometimes providing multiple ignition sources". City CWPP (p. 14): the CAL FIRE / USFS occurrence map "indicates that more wildfires start outside Berkeley, mostly east of the City boundary"; its hazard map gives "Probable Ignition Sources" a 20% weight (p. 16) but does not map or list them. EBRPD (WHRRMP Introduction, 2010, p. 9): "over 95 percent of the wildfires in the East Bay are controlled at a small size". No Berkeley ignition dataset by location was found. | **pending: fire families**: points the families at roadsides and turnouts on Grizzly Peak Blvd, Wildcat Canyon Rd, Fish Ranch Rd and SR 24, and at power lines on Red Flag days. |
| FF11 | **Recent fire starts (derived).** NIFC WFIGS Incident Locations (services3.arcgis.com/T4QMspbfLg3qTGWY/arcgis/rest/services/WFIGS_Incident_Locations/FeatureServer/0, box 122.30–122.17 W, 37.83–37.93 N, queried 2026-11-02): 22 incidents, 2019-09 to 2026-09, every one 1.2 acres or less where a size is given, cause "Unknown" or "Undetermined". They cluster on roads: 5 named "Fish" and 1 "Tunnel" near Fish Ranch Rd and SR 24; 4 "Grizzly" on Grizzly Peak Blvd, three of them within 30 m of 37.8796 N, 122.2218 W and one near Centennial Dr (2024-07-02 UTC; a 2-acre fireworks fire was reported there on the evening of July 1, 2024, The Berkeley Scanner, news); "Wildcat", "Canyon" and "Pablo" on the Wildcat Canyon side of Tilden; "Bear" near Bear Creek Rd. 21 of 22 were discovered June–October. WFIGS holds incidents reported to federal and state systems, so it misses many city fires. | **context**: confirms the roadside pattern of FF10 on the ridge east of Berkeley; too few and too small to weight ignition points by frequency. |
| FF12 | **Summer onshore wind.** Oakland Draft VMP (p. 12): "During the day, onshore winds are from the west and travel from the Bay, up the hillslopes and canyons, to the ridgetop"; the regional prevailing wind "is from the west or northwest". EBRPD (WHRRMP Introduction, p. 6): "Summers bring overnight and morning fog along the hills until around noon, with moist mid-day winds blowing westerly in from the coast". Oakland North RAWS, June–September, 12:00–19:00 local standard time, June 1992 – September 2026 (derived, WRCC tool): of the hours with mean wind ≥ 10 mph (4,910), 91.7% blew from 150–220° and 54.8% from 190–210°; only 2.0% from 220–300° (true west) and 4.1% from 0–90°. At this ridge-top station the summer onshore wind arrives from the south-south-west, steered by the terrain, not from due west; on Berkeley's west-facing slopes it will follow the slope and canyons instead (FF4 note). Afternoon speed with wind from 180–300° (25,003 hours): under 10 mph in 85.0%, 10–15 mph in 13.3%, 15–20 mph in 1.7%, 20–25 mph in 12 hours, none higher. On 1991-10-21 the NWS forecast "AFTERNOON WINDS WILL BE WESTERLY FROM 10 TO 20 MPH", humidity 50–60% (Weatherwise 2015, p. 10, quoting Jan Null's special fire forecast). | **pending: fire families** (a summer westerly fire would use these speeds and directions). |
| FF13 | **Westerly fires in the record.** June 1929, Lake Temescal: "300 acres grassland. West wind, burned from Lake Temescal toward Skyline Boulevard and Tunnel Road" (HEF list, citing the Berkeley Daily Gazette, June 22, 1929). September 1937, Broadway Terrace: 4 homes, 700 acres, west wind, backyard fire (HEF list). August 2, 2017, Grizzly Peak Blvd near South Park Dr: about 20 acres on UC and Lab land, reported 1:05 p.m. (CBS SF, 2017-08-03; news); "the day the fire ignited had mild weather and a west wind" that pushed it away from the Lab and campus, and "A fire driven by a strong east wind on a hot day would've acted very differently" (California magazine, 2017-08-07; news). The Oakland Draft VMP lists this fire as July 2017, "West/North", 9 acres (Table 6): a **conflict** in date and size, news-only on our side. XyloPlan's South/West scenario finds "relatively few pathways" in north Alameda County (p. 11, FF2). | **pending: fire families**: a summer westerly fire is supportable as a smaller, slower fire (afternoon winds mostly under 15 mph, FF12) that runs upslope and east or north-east, away from most of Berkeley. Its threat is to homes above wildland on the west-facing slopes: Claremont Canyon (Claremont Ave, Alvarado Rd, Grizzly Peak Blvd above), Panoramic Hill above Strawberry Canyon, and Hiller Highlands / Tunnel Rd (the 1929 path). That list is our inference from slope and wind, not a source. |
| FF14 | **No City or Hills Emergency Forum statement on the westerly hazard** (searched berkeleyca.gov, the CWPP, the EMBER reports, Oakland's Draft VMP, EBRPD's WHRRMP Introduction and Hazard Assessment chapters, the HEF list and web search, 2026-11-02). The City's documents treat Diablo winds as the fire weather ("Critical fire weather occurs during warm and dry periods coupled with a north wind or cold front passage", CWPP p. 11). EBRPD's hazard modeling assumed Diablo winds for hillside parks and "a condition in which winds blow to the east" only for shoreline parks (WHRRMP App. C, PDF p. 5). | **context**: a westerly fire would be the simulator's own scenario choice; label it illustrative. |

Ignition points, tied to entry corridors (FF2) and historic origins (FF9–FF11): **ref1923**, keep the City's path and vary wind only, since it reproduces the City's scenario (S11); **northHills**, Tilden east of the Summit (current), Wildcat Canyon Rd near Lake Anza (1923, 1980 origins; WFIGS "Wildcat"), Grizzly Peak Blvd at Centennial Dr (2024 fireworks start); **eastWind**, Grizzly Peak Blvd turnouts between Centennial Dr and South Park Dr (2017 fire, WFIGS "Grizzly"), Fish Ranch Rd on the east slope (1970 origin, WFIGS "Fish"); **panoramic**, upper Strawberry Canyon (XyloPlan corridor) and upper Claremont Canyon at Grizzly Peak Blvd / Claremont Ave (1970 path). A summer westerly fire is supportable (FF8, FF13) as a separate illustrative fire that starts low by a road in Claremont Canyon or near Lake Temescal and SR 24 and runs east or north-east, upslope, in a 10–15 mph wind from about 200°–260° (FF12: the station's 190–210° plus the slope-following westerly the plans describe), threatening Claremont Canyon, Hiller Highlands and Panoramic Hill. It would be milder by every source and a poor match for "the serious case". | **pending: fire families**: points and directions are assumptions anchored to the sources named; Eric decides whether a westerly fire is added (a new scenario is reserved to the maintainer, AGENTS.md). |

Points (WGS84; each checked by `tests/families.mjs` to lie on a fire-grid cell with fuel ≥ 0.7, all on 1.0 except Panoramic Hill's current point at 0.7): **ref1923**, the City's point only (3 members, S11); **northHills**, Tilden east of the Summit (current), Wildcat Canyon Rd about 150 m north-east of the shore of Lake Anza, 37.8981 N, 122.2470 W (FF9: 1923 and 1980 origins; FF11 "Wildcat"; lake outline from OpenStreetMap, way 10247089, via Nominatim), and Grizzly Peak Blvd at Centennial Dr, 37.8830 N, 122.2458 W, the network junction (FF11: 2024 fireworks start) (9 members); **eastWind** / **eastWindDay**, wildland near Grizzly Peak Blvd (current) and Fish Ranch Rd on the east slope, 37.8637 N, 122.2168 W, the midpoint of the network's upper Fish Ranch Rd segment, about 500 m south-east of its junction with Grizzly Peak Blvd and 55 m lower (FF9: 1970 origin; FF11 "Fish") (6 members); **panoramic**, above Panoramic Hill (current) and Grizzly Peak Blvd at Claremont Ave, 37.8684 N, 122.2179 W, the network junction at the head of Claremont Canyon (FF9: 1970 path; FF2) (6 members). The northHills Centennial point lies about 330 m from eastWind's current point: the two fires share a start area but keep their own orders (R09 and R05). Fish Ranch Rd's lower segment reaches the grid's east edge, so the point sits on the upper segment. | **applied**. Exact points are our assumptions anchored to the sources named; no source gives where the next fire will start, and members are not weighted. |

Share of the unclipped fire's burned area (whole run) that the clip removes: 30°, low 27% [24–29], medium 58% [51–59], high 78% [76–78]; 45° (today's fire), 0% [0–0], 26% [26–27], 72% [70–74]; 60°, 13% [13–14], 40% [39–41], 72% [70–73]. ## Other cities' orders as the fire nears (Eric, 2026-11-02)

Question: fire families (PR #35, FF18) showed fires starting at Fish Ranch Rd or Claremont Ave burning into Oakland zones no order covered, and the east-wind fire leaving people in unordered zones. Eric, 2026-11-02, in a Claude Code session: "if oakland residents are going to add to the traffic load then yeah, we have to account for it. Likewise in Kensington."

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| FO1 | **The rule (Eric's decisions, 2026-11-02, Claude Code session).** Every fire, including each fire's usual start and today's runs, not only fire-family members. Trigger: an evacuation zone is ordered once the fire comes within 2 km of it, after the simulator's default decision delay (10 min). Scope, corrected the same day: only zones of other jurisdictions. Berkeley's orders stay the scenario's City-defined region, so the 1923 repeat still reproduces the City's scenario and the Expert setting "…plus any zone within N km of the fire" keeps its meaning for Berkeley. No source gives the distance or the delay at which Oakland, El Cerrito, Kensington, Albany or the County order a zone; 2 km is Eric's choice. | **applied** (`js/sim.js` `otherCityOrders`; `data/assumptions.json` `orders`): model rule, with `firePathKm` 2 and `firePathDelayMin` 10 labeled assumptions. |
| FO2 | **How the rule is built (our choices).** (a) Jurisdiction from the Genasys zone id prefix (the layer has no jurisdiction field; the `zoneId` country-county code only separates Alameda from Contra Costa). Berkeley: `BER`, and `LBL` (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory), because the City's regions order Lab zones (Time Study R02 lists LBL-E001 to E006, p. 6-6). Other jurisdictions: `OKL` Oakland, `ALB` Albany, `EMV` Emeryville, `ECK` El Cerrito and Kensington (ECK-007 and ECK-008 are named Kensington, X7), `RCH` Richmond, `ORI` Orinda, `CCC` unincorporated Contra Costa (Tilden and the ridge). (b) Distance: straight line between 30 m cell centers, a circle (`js/fire.js` `diskMinFilter`); the zone's time is the earliest over its cells. The fire is the fire front (`fire.arrival`), as for the plan's distance setting; embers do not trigger it. (c) Delay: fixed at 10 min, the same under every plan. A plan's order delay ("Decide faster": 20 or 5 min) is Berkeley's decision speed and changes only the scenario's orders; with the plan's delay, Plan A's "first order 20 min after ignition" would also hold back Oakland. A zone the scenario already orders (R02's OKL-E001/E002, R04's OKL-003 to 005) keeps its time unless the rule's is earlier (only under a 20-min plan). (d) The rule only adds orders or brings one forward. A scenario warning that comes before the rule's order stays, and the warned households that heed it (50%, E8) leave on it; before, a later order replaced an earlier warning, so that change also applies to zones a plan's distance setting or hill order adds. (e) Like-for-like draws: the shared random stream follows the City-defined orders only (`scenTold`, unchanged); households in rule-ordered zones take supplementary draws, as households in zones a plan adds do, and carless households pick their ride among every neighbor with a car in such a zone, so a neighbor who leaves early is replaced as elsewhere. Households, parked cars and the shared stream are therefore identical for every plan and every family member (`tests/plan-draws.mjs`, `tests/families.mjs`). (f) Evacuation time (ETE, `clearP50/90/100`) counts cars from the zones the scenario and the plan order, from Berkeley's first order, as the Time Study measures a region; cars from zones only another city orders are on the same roads and slow it, but are counted separately (`otherCityVehicles`, and `clearP90All` over both). Shadow evacuation, I-80 through traffic and police start from Berkeley's first order, as before. | **applied**. (a), (c) and (f) are our choices and are stated in Assumptions & limits; (c) and (f) keep every Simple-view question's meaning. |
| FO3 | **What the rule adds, by fire and city** (derived, `node tools/other-city-orders.mjs`, today's plan, seeds 1–3, median; residents are the synthetic households placed in zones only the rule orders, × persons per household (2.37); vehicles are those that set off within the run). Usual starts: **east wind** (from 60°) low 590 residents and 270 vehicles (Oakland 462; Orinda and Contra Costa ridge zones 130), medium 3,510 and 1,169 (Oakland 483 in 6 zones; Albany 2,896, ALB-006 south-east Albany, ordered about 173 min after ignition in seed 1; ridge zones 130), high 10,049 and 3,680 (Oakland 2,699; Albany 7,091 in 3 zones); **1923 repeat** 3,292–3,474 residents and 908–1,643 vehicles at every severity (Albany ALB-006 2,896, Oakland 310–448, ridge zones 85–130); **Panoramic Hill** low 15,282 and 4,991, medium 19,150 and 7,820 (all but 130 in Oakland: Claremont Hills, Hiller Highlands, Upper Rockridge, Rockridge and, in seed 1 by about 100 min, Bushrod), high 31,933 and 12,819 (Oakland 29,447; Emeryville and ridge zones 2,481); **north hills** low 13,921 and 4,697, medium 26,385 and 8,908 (Kensington 1,960 in ECK-007 and ECK-008, ordered 10 min after ignition; El Cerrito 6,482; Albany 17,853 in 7 zones, ordered 58–236 min after ignition in seed 1; Oakland 19), high 33,490 and 14,152. Family members, medium severity: east wind from Fish Ranch Rd 18,095–23,506 residents (Oakland 17,813–23,226) and 8,613–11,140 vehicles; from Grizzly Peak Blvd at 30° 13,073 (Oakland 12,947), at 45° 1,586; Panoramic Hill from Claremont Ave 19,012–24,385 (Oakland) and 9,017–11,160 vehicles, from upper Strawberry Canyon 16,545–22,112; north hills from Lake Anza 5,408–20,510 (Kensington 1,960; Albany 2,896–12,758), from Tilden 18,100–30,632, from Centennial Dr 1,671–13,170 (Oakland). Berkeley and Lab zones: none, by construction. The 2 km reaches far into the flats: Albany and Rockridge zones are ordered while the fire front is still in the hills, because a zone's nearest cell is what counts. **Fire reach outside the ordered zones, re-measured** (FF18 method, `node tools/fire-families.mjs`, seeds 1–3, medium severity): every cell the fire front reaches in the first 2 h now lies in an ordered zone except in Berkeley and Lab zones. East wind from Fish Ranch Rd: 0 residents outside orders (was 1,066–1,355); its usual start 132 cells, about 3 residents (was 309 and 8; Lab zones). Panoramic Hill from Claremont Ave: 0 (was 651–878); its usual start unchanged (633 residents, Berkeley's BER-055, -057, -060, which R04 warns or leaves out). North hills from Tilden and Lake Anza: 0 (was about 1, Tilden parkland CCC-140); from Centennial Dr 2–3 (Lab zones). 1923 repeat: 0 (was 1–2, CCC-154). High severity still leaves Berkeley zones outside the orders: east wind usual start about 435 residents (BER-038 to -043), north hills from Centennial Dr about 3,175 (BER-041, -042, -055, -056), Panoramic Hill's usual start about 6,800. A cell the front reaches before its zone's order counts as ordered; 1–33 such cells per run, mostly the first minutes around the ignition point. | **measured**. The people FF18 found in unordered Oakland zones are now ordered; what stays unordered is Berkeley's, where the scenario's region is the City's choice. |

Without the rule, 2,522 of the 25,059 R02 cars in seed 1 first counted as out on entering OKL-003; once OKL-003 is ordered, entering it no longer counts, and those cars count as out only farther on (BER-055, BER-056 and nodes in no zone gain about 742, 392 and 461). Not seed noise: 1 km is above rule-off in 9 of 10 runs. **Berkeley's parts do not move:** Berkeley and Lab people at home (Panoramic Hill 4,283–4,465, north hills 4,958–5,849 by seed) and in unordered Berkeley zones are the same within 4 people under all three, and road-exposed vehicles from Berkeley zones change by at most 56 (about 1%), so other cities' cars do not hold Berkeley households back, and the warning-then-order handling does not change who leaves in Berkeley. **The rise is other cities' ordered people counted twice.** At high severity embers reach 1.5 km ahead of the front, so at 1 km many other-city zones are ordered after embers reach them. Their households are then counted at home (`exposedHome`) and, once they drive out under embers, again on the road (`exposedRoad` counts every vehicle that is ever on a street under embers, converted to people). Unordered, the same people were counted once, at home (`notOrderedExposed`). Seed 3, north hills: other-city people at home 0 → 4,925 while those in unordered zones fall 5,636 → 0; road 6,479 → 10,037. People counted both at home and on the road, from vehicles of households counted at home: Panoramic Hill rule off 3,788–4,112, 1 km 5,344–7,717, 2 km 3,998–5,477; north hills 4,128–4,971, 7,156–8,443, 4,935–6,596. Without that double count, every variant is below rule-off in every run (Panoramic Hill 1 km 42,253–48,089 against 43,294–51,467; north hills 21,321–32,969 against 22,471–34,877), and 2 km lower still. The double count predates the rule: about 3,800–5,000 people in these runs, nearly all Berkeley's, are counted at home and again on the road with no other-city orders at all. | **decided and applied (FO8)**: Eric chose to count each person once, 2026-11-02. |
| FO8 | **Each person counted once (Eric's decision, 2026-11-02, Claude Code session, after FO7).** "People still there when embers arrived" counts each person where they were when embers first reached them: still at home (ordered with a car, without a car, or in a zone with no order), or on the road, meaning gone from home before embers reached their street but in a car (its own, the neighbor's it rides with, or its commuter's) that drove a street under embers. A household still at home is no longer counted again when its car drives out. Employees' and visitors' cars count on the road as persons per household / cars per driving household, as before. "…when the fire front arrived" had the same double count and is counted the same way. `js/sim.js` `summary()`; `tests/exposure-once.mjs` recounts distinct people from the run's state and checks that the parts add up to them. Today's plan, S6 (the mitigation table's baseline), seeds 1–5, median, before → after: embers, east wind low / medium / high 168 / 5,276 / 46,838 → 111 / 4,340 / 39,355; 1923 230 / 4,894 / 32,845 → 156 / 3,538 / 21,407; Panoramic Hill 1,431 / 14,953 / 49,573 → 1,183 / 12,066 / 40,932; north hills 139 / 4,744 / 35,691 → 107 / 3,510 / 26,259. Figures for this measure in rows dated before this one used the double count. | **applied** |

## Hayward Fault earthquake and fire (public sources and a network check), 2026-11-02

Question (Eric, 2026-11-02): an earthquake that starts a fire, as a scenario more interesting than a joint wildfire and Bay flood. Design note: `docs/design/earthquake-fire.md`. Research only; no model change.

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| EQ1 | **USGS HayWired scenario.** A magnitude 7.0 (ShakeMap: M7.05) earthquake on the Hayward Fault "occurring on April 18, 2018, with an epicenter in the city of Oakland" (USGS data release doi:10.5066/P9LMGHRV, ScienceBase item 5d83a79be4b0c4f70d067ae0, description). | The scenario earthquake for the design note. |
| EQ2 | **California Geological Survey fault data,** services.gis.ca.gov GeoscientificInformation/Fault_Lines MapServer: layer 0 "Historic Fault" (Hayward fault zone, northern Hayward section, 2 features in the Berkeley envelope) and layer 4 "Earthquake Fault Zone of Required Investigation" (Alquist-Priolo; Oakland East, Oakland West and Richmond zones). Saved as `data/raw/cgs/historic-fault-traces.json` and `earthquake-fault-zones.json` with `.retrieved` files. The service description: the Alquist-Priolo Act "was passed in 1972 to mitigate the hazard of surface faulting to structures for human occupancy." The USGS Quaternary fault database (earthquake.usgs.gov Qfaults MapServer layer 4) gives the same 69 crossings (EQ4). | Inputs to `tools/fault-zone-check.mjs`. The zone polygons are simple bands (57 vertices for the three zones), as Alquist-Priolo zones are drawn. |
| EQ3 | **HayWired ShakeMap grid** (earthquake.usgs.gov/product/shakemap-scenario/ushaywiredm7.05_se/us/1484100039013/download/grid.xml; grid spacing 0.0167°, about 1.5 by 1.8 km). Nearest node: Second St at Gilman St MMI 8.39, PGA 40.7 %g; I-80 at University Ave MMI 8.58, PGA 35.6 %g; Grizzly Peak Blvd at Spruce St (the network node where they meet, −122.2696, 37.9043) MMI 9.70, PGA 71.9 %g. An earlier draft gave 8.91 and 53.7 %g there, read at a point 1.9 km south on Cragmont Ave (Codex review of PR #39). | Derived. Shaking is severe across the city at once. Dark signals and lost power citywide are this design's assumption, not something the ShakeMap shows; water supply is open (design note, section 6). Raw grid not committed (2.5 MB). |
| EQ4 | **Bound 1, mapped trace (derived).** `node tools/fault-zone-check.mjs trace`: 69 vehicle segments (10.9 km) cross a mapped Hayward trace. With them closed, 1,514 of 1,617 street nodes in Fire Zones 2–3 stay in one piece with exits on every grid edge. Routes to the Bay-side (west) edge from four start points, each the network node where the two named streets meet: Grizzly Peak Blvd at Spruce St 6.2 km via Spruce St, Arlington Ave, San Fernando Ave and Thousand Oaks Blvd; Cragmont Ave at Euclid Ave 6.5 km by the same gap; Claremont Ave at Grizzly Peak Blvd 13.3 km, north along Grizzly Peak Blvd and Wildcat Canyon Rd, then the same gap; Panoramic Way at Mosswood Rd none. The routes west all pass through a gap in the mapped trace near Arlington Ave and Thousand Oaks Blvd; whether the trace is continuous there is open. An earlier draft gave 7.1 km from a point 1.9 km off on Cragmont Ave (Codex review of PR #39). | Lower bound for option A. |
| EQ5 | **Bound 2, Earthquake Fault Zone (derived).** `node tools/fault-zone-check.mjs zone`: 551 vehicle segments (66.3 km) touch the zone. With them closed, the Fire Zones 2–3 nodes split: 711 in a piece whose exits are on the grid's east (22) and north (5) edges only, 459 in a piece with exits on every edge including 15 on the Bay side, 391 on segments that are all closed, the rest in small pieces. None of the four start points (EQ4) reaches the Bay side: Grizzly Peak Blvd at Spruce St and Claremont Ave at Grizzly Peak Blvd have no route, and Panoramic Way at Mosswood Rd and Cragmont Ave at Euclid Ave are cut off, every street segment at them closed. The check keeps each start fixed; an earlier draft moved a start whose streets had all closed to the nearest node still open and reported Cragmont keeping a 6.2 km route (Codex review of PR #39). | Upper bound for option A. The zone marks where the State requires a fault investigation before building. |
| EQ6 | **HayWired fire following earthquake.** Scawthorn, C., 2018, "Fire following the HayWired scenario mainshock", chap. P of SIR 2017-5013-I-Q (doi:10.3133/sir20175013). As summarized by SPA Risk (sparisk.com, conference paper 11NCEE): about 450 large fires, with burned residential and commercial floor area equivalent to more than 52,000 single-family homes. Chapter P itself could not be retrieved (pubs.usgs.gov paths tried returned 404). The data release (EQ1) gives fire damage by fire station area as Voronoi polygons. | Secondary; to confirm against chapter P before any number reaches the simulator. Candidate input for option C. |
| EQ7 | **CGS liquefaction zones** (services.gis.ca.gov GeoscientificInformation/Liquefaction MapServer, layer 2 "Liquefaction Zone of Required Investigation"). In the Berkeley envelope one large polygon covers the Bay margin and reaches into the flats east of San Pablo Ave in narrow strips; no liquefaction–landslide overlap areas in the envelope (layer 3). Not saved. | Option B; shared with the Bay Flood Explorer's low ground. |
| EQ8 | **Mutual aid after a Hayward earthquake:** see G9 (2024 LHMP p. B-51). | Option D. |
| EQ9 | **Decisions (Eric, 2026-11-03, answering Claude Code's questions in session).** (1) With the earthquake setting on, both bounds are shown: mapped trace (EQ4) and Earthquake Fault Zone (EQ5). (2) Asked whether to test an ordinary wind, Eric replied: "Doesn't the earthquake create a radically different ignition point? If so then it's important to disclose and show that." It does: fires after an earthquake start in buildings, several at once (EQ6), and the dry east wind is rare (FF5: 7,340 hours from 0–90° with humidity ≤ 25% in June 1992–October 2026, about 2.5% of hours, derived). | **applied** to `docs/design/earthquake-fire.md` ("Where an earthquake fire starts", "Decided"): option A must say on screen that it shows wildland starts on an east wind; option C (HayWired starts, onshore wind as the main case, east wind as the worst) moves up to second. Liquefaction sharing stays open. |

## Household mix by place and assisted-evacuation demand: ACS, licensing records and HHS emPOWER, 2026-11-03

Files: `data/raw/census/acs-demand.json` (Census Reporter API, release ACS 2024 5-year, retrieved 2026-11-03; `tools/fetch-demand-acs.mjs`), `data/raw/facilities/facilities-by-zone.json` (zone aggregates only; `tools/fetch-demand-facilities.mjs`), `data/raw/hhs/empower-zip.json`, and the derived `data/model/demand-by-zone.json` (`tools/build-demand.mjs`). User-Agent `berkeley-wildfire-simulator/0.1`. No household-level data, names or addresses are stored. Design for using the counts: `docs/design/assisted-evacuation.md`; run comparison: `docs/demand-by-place.md`.

| # | Insight | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| DM1 | **ACS tables fetched.** ACS 2020–2024 5-year estimates and 90% margins of error for the 201 block groups and 80 tracts of the 2020 blocks in `blocks-2020.json`: block groups B25044 (tenure by vehicles available), B25008 (population in occupied housing units), B25010 (average household size), B11016 (household size), B11007 (households with people 65+ by household size), B01001 (sex by age); tracts B18105 (ambulatory difficulty by age), B18101 (disability by age), B11010 (living alone by age of householder). The Census Bureau API now redirects keyless requests to `missing_key.html`, so Census Reporter is the source, as in RT7. B11010 and the disability tables are not published by block group (Census Reporter: "None of the releases had the requested geo_ids and table_ids"); B11007 gives one-person 65+ households by block group instead. | **applied**: cache `data/raw/census/acs-demand.json`. |
| DM2 | **Allocation to zones (derived; one assumption).** Each 2020 block goes to the Genasys zone containing its interior point (even-odd test over the zone's merged rings, `tools/zones.mjs`; 2 blocks lie in two zones and take the first). A block group's counts split among its blocks by 2020 housing units (household tables) or population (person tables), which assumes the block group's rates hold in each block (assumption). Zone shares are ratios of summed pieces; MOEs use the Census approximations (sqrt of summed squared MOEs; proportion formula, ratio formula when the radicand is negative). Range = estimate ± MOE. 149 of 169 zones have households in their blocks; LBL-001, BER-043, OKL-007, OKL-002, RCH-046 and ALB-010 have population but no 2020 households and keep the citywide values (model rule). 5,922 people (2,701 housing units) in the block file lie outside every zone. | **applied**: `data/model/demand-by-zone.json`. |
| DM3 | **Conflict: carless households, ACS vs the Time Study survey.** ACS 2020–2024 B25044: 20.3% of Berkeley's 47,862 households have no vehicle (place 0606000; 9,710 households); our allocation gives 20.7% (19.1–22.4%) over Berkeley's 78 zones and 14.6% over the model area. The Time Study's 7.47% (E7, used as `householdsWithoutVehicleShare`) comes from its demographic survey: the study computes transit-dependent people from "the demographic survey (see Appendix D) results" (ets.txt §3.7, p. 3-9), so E7's "(ACS, Table 3-10)" label is wrong about the source. A self-selected survey can under-sample carless renters and students. The ACS also gives 2.27 persons per household (B25008/B25044; B25010 2.27) against the study's 2.37 (E5, which the study attributes to the ACS 2018–2022; the vintages differ). | **conflict recorded, not resolved**. The default keeps 7.47% and 2.37; the opt-in "by place" setting uses the ACS. Choosing between them is the maintainer's call. |
| DM4 | **Within Berkeley the carless share runs from 0% to over 90% by zone.** ACS estimates with MOE ranges: hill zones BER-007, BER-016 and BER-022 0% (upper ends 3–6%), BER-020 4.3% (0.8–7.8%), BER-001 9.1% (0.3–18.0%), BER-070 19.9% (12.2–27.6%), BER-040 26.0% (21.5–30.6%), BER-055 51.5% (38.2–64.8%), BER-054 73.4% (63.4–83.4%), BER-056 93.5% (83.6–100%; 5,442 people in 563 housing units in 2020, mostly group quarters). Persons per household run from 1.57 (BER-044) to 3.70 (BER-058, range 1.53–5.88). Block-group MOEs are wide; the ranges are kept in the file and shown in the Inspector, and the setting uses point estimates. | **applied** (opt-in). |
| DM5 | **Age.** Berkeley's zones: 7.0% (6.5–7.5%) of residents are 75 or older and 16.7% (16.0–17.4%) 65 or older; 12.2% (11.2–13.2%) of households are one person 65+ (B11007). Implied on the population the simulator places in Berkeley and Lab zones: about 8,400 people 75+ (sum of zone estimates). | **reported** (Inspector, map layer "Residents 75+ (ACS)"); no trips. |
| DM6 | **Ambulatory difficulty (tract level).** B18105: 4.3% (3.8–4.9%) of the civilian noninstitutionalized population 5 and older in Berkeley's zones (about 5,300 people on the modeled population); any disability (B18101) 10.8%. Tract shares are applied to each zone by population, so zones in one tract share a rate. A difficulty is not a need for evacuation help; the Time Study's survey gives 6% of households needing help from outside the household and 1.6% / 0.2% of people needing a wheelchair van / ambulance (ets.txt ES-6). | **reported**; no trips. |
| DM7 | **Population base.** Implied counts apply each share to `popModeled`, the Genasys estimate (which may include group quarters) cut to the model area. Under "by place" the household count is `popModeled` / the zone's persons per household, the same rule as the default with a zone value; ACS persons per household counts only people in households, so zones with dorms or care facilities get more synthetic households than they have (as the default does). | **model rule**, stated in Assumptions & limits. |
| DM8 | **Setting: Household mix (Expert view, both plans).** `world.householdMix = 'place'` (`js/sim.js` buildHouseholds): usable zones replace `personsPerHousehold` and `householdsWithoutVehicleShare` with their ACS values; vehicles taken by driving households, mobilization, ridesharing and compliance are unchanged. Draws are taken in the same pattern under both settings; only the thresholds and household counts change, so Plan A and Plan B still see the same households (`tests/plan-draws.mjs` repeats the preset, Expert and R0 checks under "by place"). People counts use each household's zone value (`hhPersons`). Default: citywide; Simple view always uses citywide. Correction after Codex review of PR #50: the R0 log line counted barred homes at 2.37 persons; it now sums each barred home's zone value (seed 1, maximum growth, 1923 repeat: 65,312 people under by place, equal to the residents added without R0; 64,132 citywide). With maximum growth, R0 in Fire Zones 2–3 reproduces the no-growth run under both mixes. | **applied 2026-11-03**, opt-in. |
| DM9 | **Map layer and reported figures.** Inspector rows for a zone: ACS carless share and persons per household (with ranges), people 75+, people with an ambulatory difficulty, one-person 65+ households, licensed facilities and schools. Map layer "Residents 75+ (ACS)": share of residents 75+ by zone in four classes. | **applied**, figures only. |
| DM10 | **CDSS Community Care Licensing.** ArcGIS layer `CDSS_CCL_Facilities` (data last edited 2026-10-23), envelope of the block file: 153 facilities; 131 kept (STATUS 3; every one also in the CCL CSV of 2025-05-25 is LICENSED there). In the model area: 12 residential care facilities for the elderly (647 licensed capacity), 12 adult residential or social rehabilitation facilities (105), 104 child care centers (5,533). In Berkeley and Lab zones: 6 RCFEs (385), 4 adult residential (80), 65 child care centers (3,511). Resource family homes (13) and family child care homes are private homes and are left out; status-6 facilities (5) are left out. One 120-bed Berkeley RCFE licensed in the May 2025 CSV is not in the current layer. | **reported** by zone. |

Measured gap ÷ `NEAR_DIST`, over 3,037 footprints with `NEAR_DIST` ≥ 10: median 0.2403 m per unit (10th–75th percentile 0.2398–0.2407). Feet would give 0.3048; feet measured in Web Mercator give 0.3048 × cos 37.87° = 0.2406. So the City's distances read about 27% high, consistent with a Near analysis run in Web Mercator. Confirmed 2026-11-04 by the layer's areas: its spatial reference is wkid 102100 (Web Mercator), and its `Shape__Area` totals 2,619,654 m² against 1,627,936 m² measured in UTM, a ratio of 1.609 against Web Mercator's 1 / cos² 37.87° = 1.605. That the Near analysis itself ran in Web Mercator remains our inference. Recorded as ISSUES.md I1.
