Runs took 13.6 minutes on 7 workers (900 runs instead of 1,380, without the edge 2322 variant). | **derived**: `data/model/egress-candidates.json`, `data/model/egress-sim.json`, `docs/egress-candidates.md`. |
| EG14 | **Rerun 2026-11-01 on the network with The Crossways opened and BER-013 at its Census count (122).** Panoramic Hill (AC-155) has 1,011 residents behind the 9.1 ft foot of Panoramic Way; AC-160-N3 and AC-134-N2 each leave 222 behind a choke (789 freed). Fire off, Panoramic Hill's cars, median of 5 seeds: 1923 orders normal 113 → 111 min with AC-160-N3 (study 1:30 → 1:30), urgent 90 → 41 (AC-160-N3), 41 (AC-134-N2), 58 (City route) (study 1:25 → 1:00); Panoramic Hill orders normal 118 → 115, urgent 74 → 37, 38, 42 (study 1:00 → 0:40). The City's route had been dropped by the duplicate check because it snaps to the same nodes as a generated Dwight Pl → Dwight Wy link; the check now keeps the City's concept. Figures in EG1–EG13 are from the previous network. | **applied**: data/model/egress-*.json and docs/egress-candidates.md regenerated. |
No simulator code or data changed, so `tests/calibration.mjs` was not rerun; `tests/headless.mjs` runs as before (2026-11-01).

## Driver information and routing: egress guidance, 2026-11-01

Eric proposed piloting "egress navigation" and then warned that routing for the system optimum could kill some people (Eric rows above). Public sources below were found and read by a research agent on 2026-11-01 (all retrieved that day); pages were fetched with a generic User-Agent. "Abstract only" or "secondary only" marks what we could not read in full. The setting's code is `js/guidance.js`; `js/sim.js` calls it from `route()`, `step()`, `enter()` and `headBlocked()` only when the setting is on.

| # | Source and finding | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| DG1 | **Waze for Cities, "Waze's role in sustainable crisis response during the Greater LA wildfires"** (case study, undated, about 2025; https://www.waze.com/wazeforcities/casestudies/wazes-role-in-sustainable-crisis-response-during-the-greater-la-wildfires). States closures on more than 5,000 road segments affecting 177,500 drives, and 195 Genasys Zonehaven evacuation zones shared, viewed by 17,100 drivers; partners Waze, 7 community editors, Genasys and Cal OES. It does not say routes avoided evacuation zones. | **applied**: the Ordinary app option learns about the fire only through closures (here, once the fire front reaches a street); zones are shown to drivers, not used as routing constraints. |
| DG2 | **Google, "Crisis-related alerts in Google Maps"** (help page, https://support.google.com/maps/answer/9985621). States an alert appears when "You select a navigation route that enters an area impacted by a crisis"; users can report closures. It does not say routes avoid fires. Secondary only (press, 2020): fire boundaries from GOES satellites, updated about hourly, piloted with Cal OES and Boulder OEM. | **applied**: app drivers keep their route until a street is closed. |
| DG3 | **Los Angeles City Council Motion 17-1407 (Koretz), 2017-12-08** (https://cityclerk.lacity.org/onlinedocs/2017/17-1407_mot_12-08-2017.pdf, scanned, read by OCR). During the Creek and Skirball fires "the navigation apps recommended routes that included streets that were within evacuation areas because the data indicated there was no traffic." Secondary only: LAist (2017-12-07) reports LAPD cautioned against the apps; no verbatim LAPD quote found. | **applied**: cited in the Ordinary app note as the documented failure mode (empty streets look fast). |
| DG4 | **California AB 2908 (Low), 2019–20, "Navigation applications: fire-prone areas"** (LegiScan, https://legiscan.com/CA/bill/AB2908/2019; summary only). Would have barred navigation apps from directing traffic within 1 mile of very high fire hazard severity zones; died in committee (last action 2020-05-05). | **context**: no California law constrains app routing near fires. |
| DG5 | **Genasys press release, "Genasys partners with Ladris to provide AI-powered evacuation modeling"**, November 2023 (https://genasys.com/press-releases/genasys-partners-with-ladris-to-provide-ai-powered-evacuation-modeling). Vendor claims: "what-if" simulation of evacuation times and traffic, for planning and traffic-control decisions. No documented deployment that gives individual drivers real-time routes was found; a Ladris pilot in Paradise could not be confirmed, and the Genasys traffic product page returned 404. | **applied**: the guidance option is labeled hypothetical ("No agency runs such a system today"). |
| DG6 | **Maranghides, A., et al. 2023, *A Case Study of the Camp Fire – NETTRA*, NIST TN 2252** (https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/TechnicalNotes/NIST.TN.2252.pdf). Executive Summary: routes that normally took 25 minutes "took several hours, if they were passable at all"; 31 temporary refuge areas held more than 1,200 civilians. Sec. 7 (pp. 72–73): responders directed traffic at intersections, sent it to roads "with no direct fire exposures," and re-directed it as conditions changed. | **context**: the real-world analog of central guidance is officers at intersections: safety first and reactive. Police signal control is a separate setting being built in parallel. |
| DG7 | **ITS Japan passable-road map, 2011 Great East Japan Earthquake** (secondary only: https://easts.info/activities/awarding/award_project/otpa2013_p3.pdf). Probe data from Honda, Nissan, Toyota and Pioneer showed which roads had been driven the day before, published on Google Maps. | **context**: a precedent for sharing which roads are passable, not for assigning routes. |
| DG8 | **Wong, S., Broader, J., Shaheen, S. 2020, *Review of California Wildfire Evacuations from 2017 to 2019*, UC Institute of Transportation Studies, doi:10.7922/G29G5K2R** (https://escholarship.org/uc/item/5w85z07g). Table 10 (p. 37): used GPS and followed its route 18.9% (2017 Northern California fires, n = 37), 18.3% (2017 Southern California, n = 175), 7.5% (2018 Carr fire, n = 254); used GPS but rarely followed it 2.7%, 4.6%, 5.5%; did not use GPS 78.4%, 77.1%, 87.0%. p. 43: at least 78.2% had GPS available; the authors suggest local knowledge explains low use. p. 56, Recommendation 13: agencies should work with navigation apps to restrict travel on dangerous roads. | **applied**: the Habit note cites 77–87% not using GPS; the share setting offers 15%. |
| DG9 | **Zhao, B., Wong, S. 2021, "Developing Transportation Response Strategies for Wildfire Evacuations via an Empirically Supported Traffic Simulation of Berkeley, California," *Transportation Research Record* 2675(12), doi:10.1177/03611981211030271** (preprint https://escholarship.org/uc/item/70p6k4rf, Sec. 4.5, pp. 22–23). A Berkeley simulation with 15% of drivers rerouting as its baseline; against it, 0%, 50% and 100% rerouting change exposed vehicles by +20%, −51% and −89%, and the evacuation time estimate (ETE) is 3.2, 2.1 and 1.2 h against 2.9 h. Losing connectivity at 30 minutes with 50% rerouting raises exposed vehicle-hours by more than 50%. Rerouting: Dijkstra on the average speed of the last 20 s, every 10 s. | **context and comparison**: the closest benchmark. Its rerouting drivers know traffic, as in our Ordinary app and Today's model; its gains from more rerouting are larger than ours (DG22); the two models differ in baseline, network and measure. Not used to tune anything. |
| DG10 | **Dugstad, A.-K., et al. 2026, "Drilling for disaster preparedness: Insights from a community wildfire evacuation exercise," *Transportation Research Part D* 154:105258** (https://gala.gre.ac.uk/id/eprint/52826/). Roxborough Park, Colorado, 2024 drill (Sec. 3.4, p. 9): 78 participants said they followed the route instructions, 22 chose the shortest route, 12 a familiar route, 10 "Other"; whether several answers were allowed is not stated, and 38.4% of observed vehicles were not recorded at the assembly point. | **applied**: share option 65% (78 of 122 answers), labeled as a drill. No real wildfire has measured compliance with official route guidance. |
| DG11 | **Brachman, M., Church, R., Adams, B., Bassett, D. 2020, "Wayfinding during a wildfire evacuation," *Disaster Prevention and Management* 29(3):249–265** (abstract only). "31 percent of evacuees took a shortest distance route to their nearest exit"; chosen routes were significantly longer. | **conflict, noted**: our Habit drivers take the shortest free-flow route to the nearest way out, so Habit is if anything better informed than the drivers Brachman et al. observed. Left as is; stated here. |
| DG12 | **Dow, K., Cutter, S. L. 2002, "Emerging hurricane evacuation issues: Hurricane Floyd and South Carolina," *Natural Hazards Review* 3(1):12–18** (secondary only: most evacuees took the interstate they knew best); **Wu, H.-C., Lindell, M., Prater, C. 2012**, *Transportation Research Part F* 15(4):445–461, and **Sadri, A., Ukkusuri, S., Murray-Tuite, P. 2014**, *Journal of Transportation Engineering* 140(1):61–69 (abstracts only: usual route against officially recommended route; no percentages verified). | **context**: familiar-route evidence for Habit; no number taken from them. |
| DG13 | **Jahn, O., Möhring, R., Schulz, A., Stier-Moses, N. 2005, "System-optimal routing of traffic flows with user constraints in networks with congestion," *Operations Research* 53(4):600–616** (read as TU Berlin Report 754-2002, https://depositonce.tu-berlin.de/items/61fc9ff9-c60d-4480-8b84-d77de3a3eac0). Sec. 4.2, Table 2: on four German networks the constrained optimum with factor 1.02 closes about two-thirds of the gap between user equilibrium (each driver picks their own fastest route) and system optimum (least total travel time); that gap was 4.5% to 15.6%. On the Friedrichshain network at factor 1.01 some users travel 9.1% longer than at user equilibrium; at the system optimum, 26.1%. Our inference: their constraint bounds path length, not time against equilibrium, so even the constrained solution leaves some drivers worse off. | **applied**: our constraint is stricter and per driver (no riskier, at most 60 s slower than the driver's own best route at that moment); results report the worst individual case, not only totals. |
| DG14 | **Roughgarden, T., Tardos, É. 2002, "How bad is selfish routing?" *Journal of the ACM* 49(2):236–259** (https://www.timroughgarden.org/papers/routing.pdf). With linear latencies, equilibrium total latency is at most 4/3 of optimal, and the bound is tight. | **context**: a check on the size of any gain from system-optimal routing; ours is far smaller (DG22). |
| DG15 | **Bish, D., Sherali, H., Hobeika, A. 2014, "Optimal evacuation planning using staging and routing," *Journal of the Operational Research Society* 65(1):124–140** (abstract only): staging (holding people back so they leave later) plus routing minimizes clearance time. **CDC Public Health Law Program** emergency-law summary (secondary only, https://www.cdc.gov/phlp/publications/topic/briefs/cmel/index.html): California courts have found absolute state immunity under the Emergency Services Act. No peer-reviewed work on the ethics of assigning evacuation routes was found. | **applied**: guidance never stages anyone (rule c). Liability is not resolved by the immunity summary; Assumptions & limits lists it among what the share setting stands in for. |
| DG16 | **Options built (model rules and assumptions, `js/guidance.js`).** Every option sends drivers to the same places as before (nodes outside ordered and warned zones and the illustrative fire's ember area) and keeps the same arrival rule, so outcomes are comparable. *Today's model* (default): unchanged. *Habit*: route tree on free-flow time, burning streets closed, rebuilt only when a street starts burning; a driver whose current street has embers switches to free-flow plus the 300 s ember penalty Today's model uses; the first-choice street full: the next of up to three options, as in Today's model. *Ordinary app*: Today's cost without the ember penalty (queues and oncoming cars, burning streets closed). *Unconstrained system-optimal*: free flow plus twice the queue and oncoming-car delay (`routing.systemQueueWeight` 1), plus Today's current-ember penalty. Share of drivers: one draw per household from a stream keyed by seed and household (non-resident cars by vehicle index); followers of Habit drive by habit and the rest by Today's model; followers of the other options use them and the rest drive by habit. | **applied**. |
| DG17 | **Constrained guidance (rules a–c), final design.** Forecast: a street counts as in the forecast ember area for a driver if the illustrative fire's embers are forecast there by the time the driver would leave it plus `routing.forecastMarginMin` (30 min, assumption), and in the forecast fire area likewise for the front; arrival times come from current queues. Forecast risk of a route: seconds driven in the forecast ember area, plus a count that outranks any ember time for each street in the forecast fire area. At each junction where a following car needs a route (once per junction per minute) guidance judges three routes along their whole path: the driver's *own route* (the Habit tree: what the driver would do without guidance), the *safest route* (a forward search from the junction on time plus 60 s per ember-second, `routing.emberSecondWeight`) and a *load-balancing route* (a tree on free flow plus twice the queue delay, plus a static ember term). (a) A route is offered only if its forecast risk is no higher than the own route's and it is either at most `routing.timeToleranceS` (60 s, assumption) slower at current queues or strictly safer; the load-balancing route is preferred only when it is as safe as the safest route; if nothing passes, guidance offers nothing and the driver keeps their own route. (b) Through (a), no offered route takes the driver further into the forecast fire or ember area than their own route. (c) No departure time changes (no staging). The forecast is the simulator's own fire, so it is never wrong; a real forecast would be uncertain. Two earlier versions were replaced after the diagnosis in DG21: the first compared the load-balancing route with the safest route instead of the driver's own route; the second judged forecast risk at the current time for every street on the route, however far ahead. | **applied**. |
| DG18 | **Households worse off (model rule `routing.worseOffDelayMin` 5).** Per household, at the end of the run: level 3 a car still on the road with no road out, 2 still there (home or road) when the fire front reached it, 1 when embers reached it, 0 none; and minutes until its last car reached safety. Worse off under B than A: a higher level, or safety reached more than 5 min later or not at all. A household that left early (before ignition) counts as safe at minute 0, level 0. Runs pair by seed; households by index, with a key over street, zone and kind that must match (`hh.veh` is not used: a commuter turned back changes it during the run). | **applied**: results panel (against Habit, or Today's model for Habit, from two background runs) and Compare (when Plan A and Plan B differ in routing). |
| DG19 | **Chance in who reaches a junction first.** The simulator serves cars at junctions in a random order drawn from the shared stream, and any change in routing changes that order. Rerunning one plan with only the serving order changed (`reshuffle()` in `js/guidance.js`, a diagnostic, not a plan setting) moves many households by more than 5 min: Panoramic Hill, central, seed 1, Habit against its rerun 2,174 households (worst 135 min), constrained guidance (first version) 519, Today's model 321. A per-household comparison of two single runs therefore mixes the routing's effect with chance. A first "beyond chance" filter that reran only Plan A was not enough: with chance filtered on both sides the first version of guidance went from 115 households to 8 on the east-wind fire, low severity, seed 1. Compare and the results panel therefore show (a) households worse off under B than A in single runs, (b) worse off under both B runs than under both A runs ("beyond chance"), and (c) A against its rerun ("chance alone"). | **applied**: Compare runs 36 simulations instead of 18 when the plans differ in routing; the results panel runs three in the background. |
| DG20 | **Robustness runs (2026-11-01).** East-wind, 1923 and Panoramic Hill fires; low, central and high severity; seeds 1–5; default plan apart from routing; each option run twice (the second with another junction serving order). Options: Today's model, Habit, Ordinary app, constrained guidance and unconstrained system-optimal at 100% share, and constrained guidance at 50%. Totals are the mean of the two serving orders; "same-seed pairs" compare those means, 15 per fire. Households worse off are against Habit, beyond chance (worse under both runs of the option than under both Habit runs). Reproduce one fire and severity with `node tests/routing.mjs <fire> <severity> 5`. Synthetic households on an illustrative fire: comparisons, not predictions. | **context**: results in DG22–DG23. |
| DG21 | **Diagnosis: why the first designs left households worse off.** (1) East-wind, low, seed 1, one-sided filter: guidance as first built 115 households beyond chance, without load balancing 18, Today's model 15; tolerance 0 s 23, 600 s 101; counting the load-balancing route's queues twice or four times 74. Two-sided filter: 8, 3 and 0. Most of the first count was chance. (2) East-wind, high, seed 1, two-sided: first version 190, without load balancing 155, forecast margin 10 min 51, margin 0 39, Today's model 10, unconstrained 25. The safety detours under the 30 min margin, not load balancing, made the difference: with a perfect forecast they cost time and rarely changed exposure. The second version measured guidance against the driver's own (habit) route: 181. (3) The 1923 fire, high, 5 seeds, second version: guidance left more people still there when the fire front arrived than Habit (median 7,970 against 7,042) and more cars cut off (1,391 against 1,262); in the first 8 runs (5 high, 3 central) up to 1,188 households beyond chance, up to 374 newly cut off. Cause: the forecast marked streets the fire reaches within 30 min of now, wherever they lay on the route, so long detours avoided near streets and met streets that burned later. The third version (DG17) checks the forecast at the time the driver would reach each street: on the same fire, seed 1, people still there when the fire front arrived 6,712 against Habit 7,021, cars cut off 1,090 against 1,125, households beyond chance 583 (98 newly cut off) against Today's model 256 (28). | **applied**: third version kept. |
| DG22 | **Results, final version (DG20 runs).** People still there when embers arrived, central severity, median of 5 seeds: east wind Habit 5,178, Today's model 5,100, Ordinary app 5,252, constrained guidance 5,141, unconstrained 5,095, guidance at 50% 5,159; 1923 fire Habit 8,147, Today's 6,068, app 7,300, guidance 6,140, unconstrained 6,207, guidance 50% 6,630; Panoramic Hill Habit 17,728, Today's 16,053, app 18,596, guidance 16,010, unconstrained 15,987, guidance 50% 16,638. 1923 fire, high: 90% clearance Habit 168 min, app 105, guidance 136, Today's 125, unconstrained 124; cars cut off Habit 1,262, app 808, guidance 1,190, Today's 949, unconstrained 953. **Effects that hold in every same-seed pair (15 of 15 on each fire):** Today's model, constrained guidance (100% and 50%) and unconstrained routing leave fewer people still there when embers arrived than Habit, on all three fires; constrained guidance leaves fewer than the Ordinary app on all three fires; on the 1923 fire every option clears 90% faster than Habit. **Not in every pair:** the Ordinary app against Habit (more people still there when embers arrived in 13 of 15 east-wind and 10 of 15 Panoramic pairs, fewer in 13 of 15 on the 1923 fire); people still there when the fire front arrived and cars cut off, for every option against Habit; unconstrained against constrained routing. On the 1923 fire the Ordinary app clears 90% faster than constrained guidance in 12 of 15 pairs (never slower): the safety detours cost time where speed decides who gets out. For scale against Zhao and Wong (DG9): our fully informed drivers (Today's model) leave 2% (east wind), 26% (1923) and 9% (Panoramic Hill) fewer people still there when embers arrived than all-Habit drivers, central severity; their 100% rerouting cut exposed vehicles 89% against a 15% baseline. The models, baselines and measures differ, so this is context, not a check. | **context**. |
| DG23 | **Does constrained guidance ever leave anyone worse off? Yes, in every run.** (Figures below are from the version before the PR #22 review; current figures in DG25.) Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] of 15 runs per fire: constrained guidance east wind 21 [7–149], 1923 fire 135 [85–591], Panoramic Hill 59 [18–450] households; worst delays 15, 122 and 53 min; at most 30, 177 (131 newly cut off) and 32 newly still there when embers or the fire front arrived, or cut off. Every other option does too: Today's model 1 [0–13], 104 [62–288], 16 [2–56]; Ordinary app 13 [1–96], 119 [69–241], 115 [10–169]; unconstrained 20 [14–34], 187 [140–397], 71 [29–258]. Chance alone (Habit against its rerun) moves 1,333, 3,516 and 2,140 households (medians), and guidance leaves 3,203, 7,604 and 5,476 households better off (single runs). Why, given the design: the rule is checked when a route is offered, at current queues, and every following car at a junction gets the same route, so the cars it sends together build queues the check never saw; on the fastest fire some of them were then cut off. The two serving orders per side filter out most but not all chance. Against unconstrained routing, the constraint does not reduce the count reliably: unconstrained routing leaves 21, 282 and 58 households worse off than guidance (medians), and guidance leaves 17, 469 and 53 worse off than unconstrained. A guarantee after the fact would need a queue forecast or a per-car assignment that spreads the cars a junction sends, which this setting does not have. | **context, reported**: the guarantee is stated as holding when a route is offered (Assumptions & limits, README); Compare flags households worse off for every routing option. Open item for Eric (HANDOFF 7b2). |
| DG24 | **Default is unchanged.** With the setting at Today's model (the default; a plan without the field), `node tests/headless.mjs`, `node tests/calibration.mjs` and `node tests/plan-draws.mjs` (180/180) print exactly what main printed (diffed, timings stripped). `node tests/routing.mjs` checks that every option sees the same households and the same shared-stream state at the start as Today's model. No calibration change. Browser check (headless Chrome, Expert view, Panoramic Hill seed 1): no exceptions or failed requests; each option changes the run (people still there when embers arrived: Today's model 16,326, Habit 18,563, app 19,140, guidance 16,318, unconstrained 16,304); the results panel shows households worse off after three background runs. Compare (Plan A Habit; Plan B constrained guidance, but also the browser's default Plan B early departure of 50% and no parking on narrow roads; Panoramic Hill, three severities, seeds 1–3; 36 simulations, 52 min on a loaded machine) showed the takeaway with the ⚠ flag, the household table and the routing row. That run exposed a bug: households that left early counted as never reaching safety, so as worse off; fixed (they count as safe at minute 0) and checked in Node (0 of 1,704 early households counted worse off, Panoramic Hill, low, seed 1). | **applied**. |
| DG25 | **Rerun after the PR #22 review (2026-11-01).** Two changes: (1) when the street guidance picked is full, the fallbacks are now the driver's own Habit options, not unchecked alternatives from the forecast tree; (2) "beyond chance" now counts a household only if it is worse off in all four actual pairings of the two runs per plan, instead of comparing each household's worst A outcome with its best B outcome dimension by dimension, which could combine a level from one run with a time from the other. Same runs as DG20. Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] of 15 runs per fire (east wind, 1923 fire, Panoramic Hill): constrained guidance 18 [5–184], 122 [75–485], 60 [9–328] households; worst delays 19, 114 and 39 min; at most 1, 90 and 10 newly cut off. Today's model 2 [0–13], 89 [46–293], 14 [2–44]; Ordinary app 16 [4–97], 122 [62–238], 97 [6–171]; unconstrained 20 [12–39], 182 [125–356], 65 [26–295]; guidance at 50% 6 [1–103], 146 [70–406], 43 [12–188]. Chance alone (Habit against its rerun) 1,332, 3,412 and 2,144; guidance leaves 3,096, 7,701 and 5,390 households better off (single runs). The DG22 effects that held in every pair still hold in 15 of 15: Today's model, guidance (100% and 50%) and unconstrained leave fewer people still there when embers arrived than Habit on all three fires, guidance fewer than the Ordinary app on all three, and every option clears 90% faster than Habit on the 1923 fire. The unconstrained-against-guidance comparison in DG23 was not rerun. | **applied**: reproduce with `node tests/routing.mjs <fire> <severity> 5`. |
| DG26 | **Rerun after the second PR #22 review (2026-11-01).** Change: a guided driver who has met embers on their street is now measured against the ember-aware Habit route they would take themselves (before, against the unalerted Habit route, so guidance could approve a route riskier than the driver's own). Same runs as DG20 for Habit, Ordinary app and guidance at 100% and 50%. Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] of 15 runs (east wind, 1923 fire, Panoramic Hill): guidance 14 [5–186], 127 [75–464], 56 [9–376] households; worst delays 23, 115 and 56 min; at most 0, 40 and 9 newly cut off. Guidance at 50%: 7 [1–102], 150 [70–366], 36 [13–213]. Guidance leaves 3,096, 7,732 and 5,382 households better off (single runs). People still there when embers arrived: guidance at 100% fewer than Habit and fewer than the Ordinary app in 15 of 15 pairs on all three fires; **guidance at 50% fewer than Habit in 15 of 15 on east wind and the 1923 fire but 14 of 15 on Panoramic Hill**, so the DG22/DG25 statement that it holds at 50% on all three fires no longer applies there. 90% clearance faster than Habit on the 1923 fire in 15 of 15. | **applied**: supersedes the guidance figures in DG25. |
| DG27 | **Rerun after the third PR #22 review (2026-11-01).** Change: when every route guidance offers at a junction would turn the car back down the street it came on, the movement code used to substitute alternatives ranked by the static forecast tree, which guidance had not checked; guidance now declines there and the driver follows their own Habit route (and its U-turn rule). Same design as DG26. Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] (east wind, 1923 fire, Panoramic Hill): guidance 14 [5–186], 152 [81–425], 60 [9–381] households; worst delays 23, 126 and 56 min; at most 0, 49 and 9 newly cut off. At 50%: 7 [1–102], 135 [60–356], 36 [13–216]. Guidance leaves 3,096, 7,705 and 5,382 households better off. People still there when embers arrived: guidance at 100% fewer than Habit and the Ordinary app in 15 of 15 pairs on all three fires; at 50% fewer than Habit in 15, 15 and 14 of 15. 90% clearance faster than Habit on the 1923 fire in 15 of 15. | **applied**: supersedes DG26's figures. |
| DG28 | **Routing with contraflow (merge with PR #19).** Guidance trees (Habit, Habit after embers, Ordinary app, guidance, unconstrained) now treat the closed inbound side of a running contraflow street like a burning street, as Today's model already did (`js/sim.js` route()). Check, 1923 fire, central, seed 1, main hill exit streets with officers: no car enters a closed link after its closure under Today's model, Habit or constrained guidance; the 7, 20 and 13 cars seen on closed links were already on them when contraflow started. Combined contraflow and routing results are not part of the DG20–DG27 runs. | **applied**. |
| DG29 | **Rerun after the fifth PR #22 review (2026-11-01).** Changes: (1) a guided car's direction onto its home street was chosen by guidance's tree without the junction check; it now takes guidance's direction only if, judged along the whole route at arrival times, it is no riskier than the direction Habit would take and at most 60 s slower or strictly safer, else Habit's direction; (2) household arrival times are kept at double precision, so a delay of exactly 5 minutes no longer counts as more than 5 (single precision turned 1,810 s and 2,110 s into 30.1666660 and 35.1666679 min). Same design as DG26. Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] (east wind, 1923 fire, Panoramic Hill): guidance 13 [4–169], 149 [84–427], 57 [9–335]; worst delays 18, 113 and 43 min; at most 1, 32 and 9 newly cut off. At 50%: 8 [1–121], 159 [70–367], 33 [8–207]. Better off: 3,098, 7,735 and 5,421. Every-pair results unchanged from DG27 (guidance at 50% against Habit on Panoramic Hill: 14 of 15). Default output unchanged (headless diffed against main, plan-draws 180/180). | **applied**: supersedes DG27's figures. |
| DG30 | **Rerun after the sixth PR #22 review (2026-11-01).** Change: at a junction, the driver's own route that guidance must not make worse is now the Habit option movement would actually take from the street the car arrived on (no U-turn back while a forward street has a route); guided options are cached per approach, and guidance offers nothing where its route would be a U-turn that movement skips. Same design as DG26. Against Habit, beyond chance, median [range] (east wind, 1923 fire, Panoramic Hill): guidance 15 [4–183], 141 [86–449], 57 [10–338]; worst delays 22, 107 and 51 min; at most 1, 37 and 10 newly cut off. At 50%: 8 [0–95], 141 [70–361], 36 [18–212]. Better off: 3,104, 7,741 and 5,371. People still there when embers arrived: guidance at 100% and at 50% fewer than Habit in 15 of 15 pairs on all three fires (at 50% on Panoramic Hill, 14 of 15 in DG26–DG29, 15 of 15 here: that pair is near the line), fewer than the Ordinary app in 15 of 15. Default output unchanged (headless diffed, plan-draws 180/180). | **applied**: supersedes DG29's figures. |
## Mitigation levers on one baseline (phase 1, data only), 2026-11-01

`node tools/mitigations.mjs` (24 minutes on 7 workers) runs every lever against one baseline and writes `data/model/mitigations.json` and `docs/mitigations.md`. Synthetic households, illustrative fires; an educational comparison, not a prediction. No simulator code or data changed.

| # | Insight (source) | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| MS1 | **Method (model rule).** Baseline `DEFAULT_PLAN` (order 10 min after ignition, status-quo parking), Time Study case S6 for every fire, fires eastWind, ref1923 and panoramic, low/central/high × seeds 1–5: 15 same-seed pairs per lever and fire, 600 runs including a control. Each lever is one table entry in the harness: a plan delta read from `js/presets.js` Plan B (leaveEarly, orderSooner, parkingEngines), edge copies from `tools/widening-core.mjs`, a link from `tools/egress-link.mjs`, or `world.retreat`. A lever holds on a measure only if it is better in 15 of 15 pairs (P19). Measures from `summary()`: people still there when embers arrived, people still home when the fire front arrived, cars cut off by fire, hill residents an engine cannot reach within 5 minutes, 90% clearance. | **applied**: `tools/mitigations.mjs`. |
| MS2 | **Lever choice.** Links: the three with the largest evacuation effect in `data/model/egress-sim.json` (CITY-1, 15 of 15 on Panoramic Hill's cars cut off; AC-160-N3 to Smyth Ln, 14 of 15; AC-193-N1, the only other link whose own area's cars cut off fell, 7 of 15 on ref1923); AC-134-N2 left out as a near twin of AC-160-N3. Widenings: WP-001 (lowest block of Panoramic Way) and WP-007 (Oak Ridge Rd), the only pinches in `data/model/widening-sim.json` with engine-reach gains in most pairs; WP-005 and WP-010 (3 of 15) left out. Buyouts: footBlock with its parked cars cleared (the engine sensitivity of RT6), top100 and panoramicUpper as in RT4. Order timing: the preset's Plan B (5 min) against DEFAULT_PLAN's 10, not the preset's 20. | **applied**; costs copied from widening.json, egress-candidates.json and retreat.json, null where unpriced. |
| MS3 | **Like for like.** All 555 lever runs drew the same households, vehicles, mobilization delays, rides and non-resident counts as their baseline run (checked before stepping, rules of `tests/plan-draws.mjs` and `tests/retreat-draws.mjs`); the control (baseline rerun) matched the baseline on every measure in 15 of 15 runs. Baseline central medians reproduce earlier figures: leave early 5,083 → 3,616 eastWind, 4,859 → 3,106 ref1923, 14,920 → 12,108 panoramic (preset text: about 5,100 → 3,600, 4,850 → 3,100, 14,900 → 12,100); engine reach 1,541 → 1,050 with one-side parking (ER, P29). | **applied**. |
| MS4 | **Timing levers.** Leave early holds on people still there when embers arrived on all three fires (−1,468, −1,753, −2,812 central), and on people still home at the fire front and cars cut off on ref1923 and panoramic; 90% clearance −1 to −2 min where reached. Deciding faster (10 → 5 min) holds on people still there on all three fires (−330, −335, −390) and on people at the fire front on ref1923 and panoramic. Neither changes engine reach. | **applied** in `docs/mitigations.md`. |
| MS5 | **Street levers act only through engine reach.** One-side parking holds on engine reach on every fire (1,541 → 1,050). A red curb, widening, or buying out the lowest block of Panoramic Way with its curb cleared each give 1,541 → 766, better in 12 of 15 pairs and equal in 3 (the seed where no parked car blocks it), never worse. Widening Oak Ridge Rd holds (1,541 → 1,493). None holds on people still there, people at the fire front or cars cut off; their central changes are a few dozen people either way. | **applied**. Red curb $200–$600 against widening $240k–$5.83M for the same engine result (WD5, WD8). |
| MS6 | **New links also open an engine route around the foot of Panoramic Way**, which step 2 did not measure (EG14 "not done: engine-access change per link"): AC-160-N3 gives 1,541 → 766, the same as a red curb; CITY-1 gives 981; AC-193-N1 none. CITY-1 holds on cars cut off on the Panoramic Hill fire (195 → 155); AC-160-N3 is better in 14 of 15 (195 → 180). Neither changes people still home at the fire front (EG10). | **applied**; a finding for the next step 2 rerun. |
| MS7 | **Buyouts.** top100 and panoramicUpper hold on people still there, people at the fire front and cars cut off on the Panoramic Hill fire only (panoramicUpper: cars cut off 195 → 48, people at the fire front −315), at $114M–$319M and $298M–$731M; part of each drop is the bought-out people no longer counted (RT5). On eastWind and ref1923 they hold on nothing (panoramicUpper's engine reach, 1,541 → 981, is better in 12 of 15 pairs and equal in 3; MS9). | **applied**. |
| MS8 | **Not run.** R0 changes nothing without future growth, so it is not comparable on today's population (R1–R8). Power shutoff is a hazard condition (a scenario setting), not a mitigation; its effects are in CF14. | **open**. |
| MS9 | **Comparison precision (Codex review, PR #23, two rounds).** The first batch rounded each run's measures to 0.1 before the paired comparison, so differences under 0.05 (e.g. a 5-second slower 90% clearance, 91.75 against 91.83 min) counted as equal. Runs now keep full precision, and the batch was rerun (600 runs). Full precision exposed floating-point noise instead: engine reach is household counts × persons per household, so the same count can differ by about 4e−12 between runs (panoramicUpper seed 4: 981.1800000000003 against 981.1799999999967), which briefly showed panoramicUpper as better in 15 of 15. Pairs within a relative 1e−9 now count as equal; summaries were recomputed from the saved runs (`--from-runs`). Net changes from the first batch: several 90% clearance and people-still-there cells gain one or two "worse" pairs (e.g. deciding faster, eastWind clearance: 13 better, 1 worse); no lever gains or loses a "holds". The foot-of-Panoramic levers and panoramicUpper stay at 12 better, 3 equal, 0 worse on engine reach. | **applied**: `data/model/mitigations.json`, `docs/mitigations.md`. |
| MS10 | **Operations levers added (after PRs #19 and #22 merged), full batch rerun 2026-11-02 (16 levers plus a control, 780 runs, 16.7 min; all 735 lever runs drew the same households as their baseline, and the control matched in 15 of 15).** Contraflow on the main hill exit streets, officer-staffed and with battery-backed signs; police control of the 44 pre-timed signals; constrained egress guidance with every driver following. None holds on any measure on any fire. Contraflow: cars cut off worse in 13 of 15 pairs on east wind (51 → 60) and 12–13 of 15 on Panoramic Hill (195 → 215–216), better in at most 1; people still there when embers arrived change by under 1% either way. Police control: no measure moves the same way in more than 10 of 15 pairs. Guidance against today's plan (whose drivers are idealized and well informed): people still there when embers arrived higher in 13–14 of 15 pairs (east wind 5,083 → 5,113; 1923 fire 4,859 → 4,967; Panoramic Hill 14,920 → 14,983); against Habit drivers it is lower in every pair (DG30). The 12 earlier levers reproduce MS4–MS7 exactly (same seeds, same households). | **applied**: `data/model/mitigations.json`, `docs/mitigations.md`; the Simple view flags changes that are worse in most runs and never better. |
| MS11 | **Simple view redesign (Eric, 2026-11-02).** Eric: "the explanation of mitigations is categorically different from running a simulation"; "we should not tell them what the results of mitigating are. We want them to engage with the idea of mitigation which they will not do if we just declare what the answer is"; mitigations "can be stacked", so picking them "queues up a new simulation run"; changes "too unstable to simulate … probably aren't candidates for implementation anyway. So we show them, greyed out, and explain that these are not reliably beneficial." Rule applied: a lever is greyed out on a fire when no measure did better in all 15 stored pairs or in at least three of four and never worse (the earlier card rule); on every fire that is contraflow (both), police control, guided routing and link AC-193-N1. A side note (2026-11-02) warned that a single run can make a bad change look good, so a comparison always runs all 15 pairs and only the full set earns a verdict. Stacking reproduces the stored single-lever runs exactly (`tests/stack.mjs`: 8 levers, east wind central seed 2). | **applied**: `js/main.js`, `js/mitigate.js`, `js/levers.js`, `js/worker.js`; `js/sim.js` world.retreat.clearParking may list segments, so a stacked foot-block buyout clears only its own curb. |
| MS12 | **Try-changes presentation (Eric, 2026-11-02).** Eric: "tiny differences can be noise"; "'runs' -> 'passes'"; "we should consistently show results on the right and controls on the left"; "'Results' -> 'Outcomes'"; Compare and Watch-yours disabled until a change is picked. Rule applied (**model rule, not a measured uncertainty**): in a Try-changes comparison a pass counts as better or worse only when the two differ by more than 1% of the larger value; smaller differences count as "within 1%" and the verdict says how many. 1% is a presentation threshold chosen for readability; the simulator's actual run-to-run spread varies by measure and fire and was not used to set it (same-seed pairs share households, so their differences are not sampling noise in the usual sense). Small counts (cars cut off, tens of cars) still register single-car differences as more than 1%. A pass runs both plans on one fire draw (severity × seed). Vocabulary (Eric, 2026-11-02: "so there's a fire scenario and a plan and we are testing the plan against that scenario"): **fire scenario** = what Berkeley does not control (ignition, wind, severity, seed, time of day, and the order area that goes with the fire); **plan** = what it does control, changes made before the fire (parking, curbs, widening, roads, buyouts) and the response on the day (orders, leaving early, traffic management). The second tab, first called "Try changes", is "Change the plan" (Eric: "'try changes' is thus trying changes to the plan or adjusting the plan or something succinct"); its checklist is grouped by those two halves. The first tab, first called "Watch a fire", is "Today's plan" (Eric: "Change the plan also lets you watch a fire. So the first tab is really about watching the current plan and only the current plan"). One set of playback controls (Eric: "can this be consolidated?"): in Simple view the Run / Restart / Speed / slider block moves into the open tab and the separate "Playing on the map" box is gone; the Today's plan tab always shows today's plan. "Play" is "Run" (Eric: "'Play' is too, well, playful for this topic. Run is better"). Severity "central" is shown as "Medium" (Eric: "severity is 'central' and not 'medium' … is this someone else's language?"; it was not: modelers' jargon from the first prototype, not City or Time Study wording). The key stays `central` in code and data, so stored runs are unchanged; the tooltip says Medium is calibrated to the StoryMap 1923 repeat timing (S11) and low and high are half and double. | **applied**: `js/main.js` (`NOISE`, `tolSame`, triesHTML; `#try-out` in the right-hand panel), `index.html`, `css/style.css`. The stored mitigation table and the grey-out rule (MS11) keep their exact comparison. |
| MS13 | **North-hills fire added to the table, 2026-11-02** (P30). `node tools/mitigations.mjs --fires northHills --merge` (new `--merge` option: runs only the fires named, keeps the saved runs of the others, takes the saved seeds and levers, and recomputes summaries from all runs): 260 new runs, 6.1 min on 7 workers; 1,040 runs in all. The 780 saved runs of eastWind, ref1923 and panoramic were copied unchanged (every run object identical, and all 51 of their summaries and their baselines identical to before). All 980 lever runs drew the same households as their baseline; the control matched in 20 of 20. On `northHills` (central medians): leave early holds on people still there when embers arrived (4,764 → 3,295), people still home at the fire front (254 → 107) and 90% clearance (92.3 → 91.6 min); deciding faster holds on people still there (4,764 → 4,494); one-side parking and the Oak Ridge Rd widening hold on engine reach, as on every fire. Greyed out in Try changes (rule of MS11): the Buena Vista Way link, contraflow (both), police control and guided routing, the same five as on the other fires. Contraflow leaves more cars cut off in 8 of 15 pairs (fewer in none); guidance leaves more people still there in 10 of 15. | **applied**: `data/model/mitigations.json`, `docs/mitigations.md` (prose extended to four fires). |
| MS14 | **Break down this plan (Eric, 2026-11-02: "when I stack 'try changes' options -- is there a way to say which element of the stack had which effect or are they too interlinked? It's not great if people have to try multiple combinations to discover serious candidates"; then "build the break down this plan button").** Under a finished comparison of two or more changes, a button runs, for each change, the change alone and the plan without it, on the same passes (2 × changes × 15 runs, minus any already run; results are cached per fire, stack, severity and seed). Rules (**model rule**): "does its own work" = the plan is worse without it in every pass on some measure; "helps alone, but adds little on top of the rest" = alone it is better in every pass, or in at least three of four and never worse, on some measure, and the first rule fails; the overlap is named as the other changes in the plan that also help alone on one of those measures; a measure is listed only when alone or without-it is better or worse in at least three of four passes and never the other way. Not an exact attribution (Shapley values would need every subset). Check (east wind, leave early + red curb + Smyth Ln link): leave early does its own work (people still there when embers arrived 5,083 → 3,615 alone; plan 3,635 → 5,083 without it, 15 of 15); the red curb and the link each take engine reach 1,541 → 766 alone and are named as overlapping each other. | **applied**: `js/main.js` breakdown, breakdownHTML; 90 extra simulations took 43 s in headless Chrome. |

No simulator code or data changed (`js/`, `data/assumptions.json`, `data/model/network.json`), so calibration is unchanged; `tests/headless.mjs` was rerun.

## Weekday non-resident placement: workplaces, parking, schools and signals (public data), 2026-11-03

Opt-in scenario setting "Weekday non-resident placement: by workplace" (`world.nrPlacement`, `js/sim.js` `workplaceAreas`; inputs from `node tools/workplaces.mjs` → `data/model/workplaces.json`). Results and method: `docs/weekday-placement.md`.

| ID | Insight (source) | Status |
|---|---|---|
| WP1 | **LEHD LODES8, California, job year 2023** (latest in LODES8; WAC `ca_wac_S000_JT00_2023`, OD `ca_od_main_JT00_2023`, crosswalk `ca_xwalk`; lehd.ces.census.gov, retrieved 2026-11-03; extract `data/raw/lodes/wac-od-2023-model-area.csv`, 1,682 blocks with jobs in the model area). Berkeley workplace blocks: 1,075 blocks, **73,292 jobs**. | **applied** as spatial weights only: where employees work, within each Time Study area. |
| WP2 | Of the 72,874 Berkeley jobs in the OD main file (California residents), **63,101 (86.6%)** are held by people whose home block lies outside Berkeley (derived). The Time Study used LODES from OnTheMap and found about 84.4% (§3.4, p. 3-6). | **context**: consistent; the study's 84.4% is kept. |
| WP3 | **LODES against Table 3-5, by area** (the model's area rule applied to each block's internal point): in-commuting jobs flats 29,524 (study employees 5,553), West Berkeley 15,467 (5,035), North Berkeley Hills 2,683 (0), South Berkeley Hills 770 (172), UC 14,582 (11,553), LBNL 75 (3,028). The study counts only employers with 100 or more staff (§3.4, p. 3-5) and LODES counts every job, so flats and West Berkeley carry 3–5 times the study's figure. By study zone, LODES puts 4,314 in-commuters in BER-E037 and 5,960 in BER-E044 where Table C-5 lists 107 and 877. | **conflict**, recorded: the study's totals by area are kept (sourced); LODES only shares them out within an area. Using LODES totals instead would add about 40,000 employees and is the maintainer's decision. |
| WP4 | **LODES puts UC Berkeley's jobs on one block.** Block 060014224001001 holds 16,638 jobs, 16,636 of them in educational services (NAICS 61), with its internal point near Oxford St, in study zone BER-E039; Table C-5 puts UC's 11,553 at BER-E043. The Lab's tract (9821) shows 83 jobs against the study's 3,028 Lab employees, so LODES does not locate Lab staff either (they are UC employees). | **applied**: that block is left out of the employee weights; UC and Lab employees are placed by parking (WP5, WP6). |
| WP5 | **OpenStreetMap off-street parking** (`amenity=parking`, 1,747 features in the model area, OSM data as of 2026-11-03 via maps.mail.ru Overpass, because overpass.kumi.systems timed out; footprints near campus and the Lab from a second query; every `addr:*` tag removed before storing, `tools/fetch-osm-parking.mjs`). Classified (model rule) as UC (operator or name names UC, or inside the City's UC neighborhood): 33 facilities, 3,448 spaces; Lab (inside an LBL zone): 45 facilities, 993 spaces; City of Berkeley garages: 1,626 spaces. Where a facility has no `capacity` tag, capacity = footprint ÷ 30 m² per space (× levels for structures) (assumption); 4 facilities with neither are left out. | **applied**: UC and Lab facilities are where those cars park under the setting. |
| WP6 | **UC's published parking total**: "5,500+" parking spaces, most reserved for University affiliates (UC Berkeley Parking & Transportation, pt.berkeley.edu/parking-0, read 2026-11-03). The 2011 Parking and Transportation Demand Management Master Plan (Nelson\Nygaard, February 2011, p. 11) gives 6,952 spaces in Fall 2009 including 554 attended spaces, a midweek peak of 5,531 occupied (80%), and 5,467 spaces after planned removals. | **applied**: OSM UC capacities scaled to 5,500 (model rule: OSM's 3,448 is incomplete, the published total is not located). |
| WP7 | **UC parking holds at most a third of UC's weekday cars in S4.** The study's UC-related vehicles are 3,563 employee + 12,110 off-campus student + 1,983 on-campus student (Tables 3-5, 3-9). In S4 (Table 6-5 factors 100%) UC employee and commuting-student cars are 15,673 against 5,500 spaces (35%); in S1 (employees 96%, off-campus students 10%) 4,631, all of which fit (derived). | **applied**: the share of these cars placed in UC parking is spaces ÷ cars, at most 1; the rest park on streets near campus as by area. On-campus students stay where they live. |
| WP8 | **CDE public schools** (school directory and 2025-26 Census Day enrollment, retrieved 2026-11-03; `data/raw/cde/public-schools-model-area.csv`): 36 active public schools with a directory point in the model area, 17,618 students; Berkeley Unified's 19 schools hold 9,352 (Berkeley High 3,279; Time Study Table 3-7: 3,427). Berkeley Unified plus the 2,252 private students in its area (WP9) is 11,604, against the study's K-12 total of 11,977 (Table 3-7). Stored as enrollment by evacuation zone (`schoolsByZone`). | **context**: the simulator has no school trips (parents who pick up children leave on the study's later departure curves, E9; school buses, E29, are not modeled), and school staff are already in LODES. |
| WP9 | **CDE private school affidavits 2025-26** (schools with 6 or more students, revised 2026-09-25): 18 private schools in Berkeley Unified's area with 2,252 students and 582 staff; Albany 1 school, 136 students. The file has no addresses, so the schools are not placed. | **context**. |
| WP10 | **OSM traffic signals** (`highway=traffic_signals`, 381 nodes in the model area, OSM data as of 2026-11-03): 242 lie in Berkeley (the City layer, G4, covers those); the other 139 snap to 119 junctions outside the city (7 left unsnapped), written to `data/model/signals-outside.json` by `tools/signals.mjs`. Control type unknown. | **context**: no setting reads them yet. |
| WP11 | **`data/model/signals.json` on main is stale.** Rerunning `tools/signals.mjs` on the current network and the City layer (146 signals, retrieved 2026-11-03) gives the same 143 signals, but 95 of the 145 stored junction node ids now name other junctions (for example node 744 is stored as 9th St / University Av and is Channing Wy / College Av in `network.json`); the network was rebuilt after the file was made. The police-control and Red Flag shutoff settings therefore act on the wrong junctions for those 95. | **pending, needs Eric**: regenerating the file fixes it and changes those opt-in settings' results (and `contraflow-streets.json` should be checked the same way); not done here because this PR must leave every result unchanged. |
| WP12 | **Signal locations against the model's junction capacities** (City and OSM signals on the current network; a junction's type is the class of its main street and the highest class of a cross street). Signals at arterial × arterial junctions: 9 City + 27 OSM, against 15 unsignalized; arterial × local: 59 + 29 against 378; local × local (County class): 73 City signals against 1,857 unsignalized. The model gives each junction one capacity by its highest street class (900 / 1,200 / 1,600 veh/h) and a fixed delay (5 / 10 / 20 s) whether or not it has a signal. 73 City signals sit where the County file classes every street Local, so those junctions get the local capacity and delay. | **context**: a signal rule (signalized junctions at the cross street's green share, plus cycle delay) is the change these locations point to; it is not tuned here (see docs/weekday-placement.md). Signal timing itself remains an information gap. |
| WP13 | **Caltrans PeMS** (pems.dot.ca.gov) needs an account; not registered. It would give 5-minute volumes and speeds from freeway detectors on I-80, I-580 and CA-24 near Berkeley, which would check the Time Study's external through traffic (E35: 47,962 vehicles over 2 h) and the weekday background load on the freeway links. City arterials have no PeMS detectors. | **gap**. |
| WP14 | **Calibration with the setting on** (`PLACEMENT=workplace node tests/calibration.mjs 3`, see "Calibration check"): weekday 90th percentiles move by 0–5 min (R02 S4 +5 toward the study, R05 S4 −1 away from it within seed noise, others 0); nights by at most 1 min. This is the sensitivity of this one rule (visitors placed as before, the study's totals kept), not a bound on what placement can do; signal timing, through traffic, visitor placement and demand totals remain open hypotheses. | **applied** as an opt-in setting (default off); turning it on by default is the maintainer's decision. |
| WP15 | **Codex review of PR #51, round 2 (blocking): three UC facilities (268 scaled spaces) had no eligible street within 300 m**, so their weight fell back to street parking: S1 placed about 95.1% of UC employee and commuting-student cars in UC parking while the log said 100%, S4 about 33.4% against 35%. | **applied**: a facility with no eligible street within `snapM` enters at the nearest eligible street at any distance, so the stated share is the share placed (`js/sim.js` `workplaceAreas`). Calibration with the setting on rerun (WP14 and "Calibration check" give the new figures); default output unchanged. |

## Goal seek ("Set a goal" in the Simple view), 2026-11-02

| ID | Insight | Use |
|---|---|---|
| GS1 | **Framing (Eric, 2026-11-02, answering Claude Code's questions in session).** "There are two paths. A user can just pick something and run it to see how it does. OR they can do a goal seek": the tool searches and shows the combinations that meet a goal. Goals: several harms at once, each stated as a cut against today's plan ("cut by X% vs today"). Fire: the one chosen, "since some actions (like changing Panoramic) don't help in a north berkeley fire". Answers precomputed so they come at once; ordered fewest changes first, then cost, with unpriced changes said to be "not priced" rather than treated as free. This is the second path beside MS11's: Change the plan still states no answers. One limit on the fire-specific reasoning: engine reach is measured at ignition over all Fire Zones 2–3 residents, so it is the same on every fire for a seed (MS1), and Panoramic Way changes do cut it on the north hills fire. | **applied**: `js/goal.js`, third Simple-view tab "Set a goal". |
| GS2 | **Changes left out stay visible (Eric, 2026-11-02: "please keep the notice that counterflow doesn't help and yes we looked at it in the goal seek results").** The search combines only changes that reliably helped alone on the fire in `data/model/mitigations.json` (the grey-out rule of MS11). The others (contraflow officer-staffed and with signs, police control, egress guidance and the Buena Vista Way link on every fire as of this table) are listed under the answers with their stored result ("tested alone on this fire in 15 passes … did not reliably help", plus any measure they made worse) and the line that they can still be added by hand in Change the plan. | **applied**: "Changes the search left out" in the answers panel. |
| GS3 | **Held-out passes (a side agent's note, relayed by Eric 2026-11-02; Eric chose to precompute them).** Checking about 200 combinations on the same 15 passes finds some that clear a target by luck, most often combinations whose cut sits close to the target. Each combination therefore also runs 15 fresh passes (low, medium and high severity × seeds 6–10) that play no part in choosing it, as calibration checks on regions not used for tuning. An answer must meet the goal in all 30; one that met it on the 15 search passes only is listed apart as "don't rely on it". Cost: the precompute doubles, about 9 h on 7 workers for four fires. | **applied**: `tools/goal-combos.mjs` (seeds 6–10), `js/goal.js`. |
| GS4 | **Rules (model rule).** A pass meets a goal on a harm when the plan's value is at most (1 − X) × today's on the same draw. Today at 0: met only if the plan is also 0. A 90% clearance never reached counts as slower than any reached. Combinations: every set of up to three of the GS2 changes, excluding pairs that set the same plan key or rewrite the same street (`js/levers.js` conflict). Answers are minimal: a combination is not shown if a smaller one inside it also meets the goal on all 30 passes. Within the same number of changes, combinations whose every change has a screening cost come first, cheapest low end first, then those with an unpriced change. Middle values shown are over the 15 search passes, as in Change the plan. The goal is checked against the default start and wind of each fire, fall night (S6), no growth. | **applied**. |
| GS5 | **Same code path as the browser.** `tools/goal-combos.mjs` builds each run with `js/levers.js` `stackOf` and `dataFor` and reads outcomes with `js/mitigate.js` `MEASURES`, as `js/worker.js` does. Check on a 60-run test (panoramic, today's plan and the first three single changes, seeds 1–5): 60 of 60 runs match `data/model/mitigations.json` on people still there when embers arrived, cars cut off, engine reach and 90% clearance (the table's "front" counts people at home only, so it is not compared). The full build repeats this check on every single change and fire and stores it in `meta.check`. | **check**. |
| GS6 | **Judge a goal at the pace picked (Eric, 2026-11-03, answering Claude Code's question in session).** The full build (all four fires, 6,120 runs each; east wind and the 1923 repeat on this Mac, Panoramic Hill and the north hills in cloud sessions; a second cloud build of east wind matched the local one exactly; 720 of 720 single-change and baseline runs agree with `mitigations.json`) showed the GS4 rule over all 30 passes left most goals unmet: at high severity no combination of up to three changes cut people still there when embers arrived by more than 7% (east wind, Panoramic Hill) to 19% (1923 repeat) in every pass, and at low severity a few cars or people make one pass decide. Best cut in every pass at the 1923 pace (medium severity): embers 16–29%, fire front 30–49%, cars cut off 36–82%, by fire. Choice: a goal is judged on the 10 passes at the pace picked in the Simple view (5 search, 5 held out), and the page says the answers change with the pace. Low severity stays in Expert view and is not offered here. No combination cut 90% clearance by more than 6% in every pass on any fire, so clearance goals return no answer except on Panoramic Hill at the 1923 pace, where buying out upper Panoramic Hill lets the hill reach 90% where today's plan does not within the run. With 5 held-out passes instead of 15, the held-out check is weaker against luck (GS3). | **applied**: `js/goal.js` (`passes`, `evaluate(fire, targets, sev)`), pace buttons on the Set a goal tab, `tests/goal.mjs`. |
| GS7 | **20 passes per pace, and the 8-block buyout (Eric, 2026-11-03: "3h is fine. Go 1-4"; "Wait should we add the other block?").** The ~100-household buyout now takes 8 blocks (RT12), so `tools/mitigations.mjs` was rerun (only `retreat-top100` changed: runs, cost, summaries) and its combinations with `--merge --only retreat-top100` (6,360 runs, 82 min). Then `--merge --add-seeds 11-20 --sevs central,high` (16,320 runs, 252 min on 7 workers) gave each Simple-view pace 10 search passes (seeds 1–5, 11–15) and 10 held-out passes (6–10, 16–20). 720 of 720 single-change and baseline runs agree with `mitigations.json`. Best cut in every pass, 10 → 20 passes, at the 1923 pace: embers 16–28% (was 16–29%), fire front 26–44% (30–48%), cars cut off 36–74% (36–82%), engine reach 91% (94%); at the 1991 pace embers 6–18%, front 12–49%, cars cut off 50–55% (51–56%). The Panoramic Hill clearance answer at the 1923 pace is gone: no plan reaches 90% clearance in all 20 passes where today's plan never does (was 10 of 10). The tool's `--only` now reruns every pass the output holds, including added seeds. | **applied**: `data/model/goal-combos.json`, `data/model/mitigations.json`, `docs/mitigations.md`, `tools/goal-combos.mjs`; the page reads held-out passes by seed (`meta.holdoutSeeds`). |

## Home-to-street walking and stair access, 2026-11-03
`node tools/walk-access.mjs` writes `data/model/walk-access.json` and `docs/walk-access.md`: hill homes' height and distance to their street, door-to-curb walking times and carless households' walks out, per street segment. Synthetic households; no building, parcel or household is stored. `node tools/walk-access-sim.mjs` compares the opt-in setting off and on.

| # | Insight (source) | Status / use |
|---|---|---|
| WA1 | **Source:** OpenStreetMap ways tagged highway=steps, footway, path or pedestrian in the model area (37.836–37.912° N, 122.334–122.206° W), Overpass API at overpass.kumi.systems, retrieved 2026-11-03; the server's data is as of 2026-06-01, five months older than the street extract (2026-11-01); © OpenStreetMap contributors, ODbL. Query `data/raw/osm/walk-ways.overpassql`, response `walk-ways.json` (5.6 MB). 8,583 ways: 6,802 footways (3,227 tagged sidewalk or crossing), 1,030 stairways, 655 paths, 96 pedestrian ways. Of the stairways, 159 carry step_count, 552 incline and 368 a handrail tag. 456 public stairways lie in the hills (Fire Zones 2–3 or the 90 m proxy outside Berkeley); named Berkeley paths such as Covert, Orchard Lane, Eucalyptus and Arbon rise 48–54 m by the DEM. | **applied**: pedestrian network, stairway length and rise, path or stair access to homes (`tools/walk-access.mjs`). |
| WA2 | **Source:** Microsoft Global ML Building Footprints, United States tile quadkey 023010212 (release 2026-02-03, uploaded 2026-02-23, 64.1 MB gzip, 621,686 footprints), from https://minedbuildings.z5.web.core.windows.net/global-buildings/2026-02-03/global-buildings.geojsonl/RegionName=UnitedStates/quadkey=023010212/part-00072-4feead82-d499-422b-94cb-c036c212127a.c000.csv.gz (listed in `dataset-links.csv`), retrieved 2026-11-03; © Microsoft, ODbL. Reduced to centroid and area for the 55,757 footprints of 25 m² or more in the model area (`data/raw/walk/ms-centroids.json`, gitignored); the tile itself is not kept. 13,591 are hill buildings (8,232 in Berkeley's Fire Zones 2–3, 5,359 outside Berkeley at 90 m or higher) against 18,729 synthetic households on the same streets. | **applied**: building positions for the street join; machine-learned outlines, labeled sourced (machine-learned). |
| WA3 | OpenStreetMap buildings could not be fetched for a coverage check: the whole-area query (`data/raw/osm/walk-buildings.overpassql`, `out tags center`) timed out or returned 502 on overpass.kumi.systems and overpass.private.coffee between 19:35 and 20:00 UTC on 2026-11-03, and a 3 × 3 tiling (`out tags center`) returned 3 of 9 tiles by 20:30 UTC, none of them over the north or east hills. In those three tiles (37.836–37.886° N, 122.300–122.235° W; server data of 2026-05-31 to 2026-07-24) OpenStreetMap has 25–30% more buildings than the Microsoft footprints of 25 m² or more (12,430 against 9,629; 11,546 against 8,875; 15,254 against 12,245), so OSM coverage is not thin there and the Microsoft counts are low. Microsoft footprints were used because OSM could not be fetched; the tile responses were not kept. | **open**: compare OSM and Microsoft counts by zone when Overpass answers. |
| WA4 | **Source:** Tobler, W. (1993), *Three Presentations on Geographical Analysis and Modeling*, NCGIA Technical Report 93-1 (https://escholarship.org/uc/item/05r820mz): walking speed W = 6·exp(−3.5·|dh/dx + 0.05|) km/h, 5.04 km/h (1.40 m/s) on the level, fastest on a 5% downgrade. The formula was checked against the R package movecost's cost-function registry, which cites the report; the report text was not read (the PDF link returned a web page). | **applied** as the walking speed on slopes (sourced). |
| WA5 | **Source:** Pouw, Corbetta, Gabbana, van der Laan and Toschi, "High-statistics pedestrian dynamics on stairways and their probabilistic fundamental diagrams", arXiv:2307.15609v2 (2024), Table 1 (horizontal free-flow speeds from the literature): Fruin (1971) on a 178 mm riser, 286 mm tread (32°) stair, 0.51 m/s ascending and 0.67 m/s descending; on a 152/305 mm stair 0.57 and 0.77. Their own field data: slow walkers 0.49 m/s up, 0.58 m/s down. Fruin's original was not read. | **applied**: central stair speeds 0.51 up, 0.67 down (horizontal, sourced); the 178/286 mm geometry sets how much horizontal run a stair needs per meter of rise (model rule). |
| WA6 | **Source:** MUTCD §4E.06, pedestrian clearance: 3.5 ft/s for clearance time and a 3.0 ft/s check for slower pedestrians (via the Delaware adoption on regulations.justia.com and the NYSAMPO signal-timing fact sheet; the federal text was not read). | **applied**: reduced-mobility level walking speed 3.0 ft/s (0.914 m/s), scaled on slopes by Tobler's factor (derived). |
| WA7 | **Source:** Kuligowski, Peacock, Hoskins and Wiess (2014), "Stair Evacuation of People with Mobility Impairments", *Fire and Materials*, doi:10.1002/fam.2247 (NIST abstract page): overall average stair speed (0.31 ± 0.16) m/s, individuals 0.07–0.91 m/s, in building evacuations including an assisted-living facility. The abstract does not say whether speed is along the stair or horizontal, or up or down (the drills are descents). | **applied** as 0.31 m/s along a 32° stair, 0.26 m/s horizontal, descending (derived; reading it as along the stair is an assumption). |
| WA8 | No public figure for stair ascent by people with mobility impairments was found (Boyce, Shields and Silcock 1999, *Fire Technology* 35, is about level walking and ramps; Fujiyama and Tyler 2010 show elderly speeds only in charts). | **assumption**: 0.20 m/s horizontal, WA7's descent speed scaled by Fruin's up/down ratio (0.51/0.67). |
| WA9 | **Rules (model rules).** Hill buildings: footprint centroid in Fire Zones 2–3 or, outside Berkeley, at 90 m or higher; footprints under 25 m² dropped (assumption). Street: nearest point of a segment that carries synthetic households (`residentialStreets`), up to 150 m (30 buildings had none); 8 buildings on DEM faults (sea-level cells, or steeper than 1:1 to the curb) dropped. Path or stair access: a public stairway or path (not a sidewalk) within 30 m and 10 m closer than the street. Route: the straight line; above a 25% slope stairs take the whole rise. Aggregates only; a segment with fewer than 5 buildings uses its zone's pooled figures (877 of 1,862 hill segments). | **applied**: `tools/walk-access.mjs`. |
| WA10 | **Findings.** Median hill building 3.0 m above or below the nearest point of its street, 90th percentile 7.6 m; 1,886 (14%) more than 5 m above and 412 (3%) more than 10 m above; 1,470 (11%) more than 5 m below and 196 (1%) more than 10 m below; 457 (3%) closer to a path or stairway than to a street, 170 of them a stairway. The steepest lots are outside Berkeley (Contra Costa CCC-154, Orinda's Monte Vista, Oakland's Merriewood and Claremont Hills zones); in Berkeley, Panoramic Hill (BER-013) leads, 16% more than 10 m above and 11% more than 10 m below. One way door to curb: median 21 s, 90th percentile 35 s (reduced mobility 34 and 67 s). The terrain excess for three legs: median 18 s, 90th percentile 44 s (reduced mobility 29 and 115 s). | **context**: `docs/walk-access.md`. |
| WA11 | **Carless walks** (expected carless households: the uniform 7.47% of synthetic households on hill streets inside each scenario's order and warning area; targets are the simulator's safe streets at medium severity, seed 1, in an evacuation zone or at a model-area exit, not upwind of the ignition). Central median and 90th percentile: 1923 repeat 13 and 23 min; east wind 7 and 18; Panoramic Hill 4 and 12; north hills 8 and 21. Reduced mobility: 20 and 36; 10 and 29; 6 and 21; 13 and 32. To the nearest of 13 shelter sites: 24 and 54 min central, 37 and 84 reduced; 508 of 1,399 expected carless hill households are more than 30 min away (842 with reduced mobility). These figures follow the round 3 to 5 fixes below. Segments with no walking route to a shelter (2.5 expected carless households with stairways and paths, 15.7 on streets only; none in the four scenarios) count as slower than any reached one in the percentiles and as over 30 and 60 min, and are reported separately; until the Codex review of PR #49 (round 1) they were dropped from the percentiles while staying in the totals. The same review found that a network path split by an OSM connection kept the path class only on its last piece, so the streets-only network carried most of Loma Steps and all of Willow Walk; every piece now keeps its edge's class (shelter, streets only: 23.9 and 53.7 min before, 24.3 and 55.4 after). Round 2 of that review found County no-vehicle segments drawn over OSM stairways (El Paseo Path, edges 2643 and 3157; edge 1064, County name Keeler Wk, on the John Muir Path route the review cited) costed as sloped walks, so routes skipped the stair cost (El Paseo 317 s with reduced mobility against 730 s on the OSM steps). Now every piece of a County path or no-vehicle segment within 6 m of an OSM stairway, or named STEPS, is costed as stairs: 4,405 of 9,899 m. Shelter walks, reduced mobility with stairways and paths: 35.8 and 83.5 min before, 36.1 and 83.5 after; 818 → 821 households over 30 min. On very steep pieces the stair speed is faster than Tobler's, so a few walks got shorter. Nowhere else does the project cost County paths as walks: the door-to-curb leg is a straight line, and the simulator does not route walkers. Round 3 found that the walk from a segment's middle to its ends was priced at level speed. Each segment's middle is now a node of the costed graph. An audit of every walking time then found three more faults, fixed in the same commit: (a) shelter sites were snapped to the nearest street node with the last walk to the site uncosted (up to 133 m), now priced by slope and direction; (b) DEM cells at 0 m (missing coverage at the grid's east edge, as `js/fire.js` reads them) and no-data cells were read as a cliff, now take the nearest valid elevation along the line (11,097 profile points; stairway rises also ignore such cells); (c) vertices under 0.5 m apart (where a line is cut) turned a DEM step into a near-vertical slope, and are now merged into the next piece. Segment 6626 (Central Park Dr), reduced mobility to a shelter: 76.3 min after round 2, 83.6 now. Round 4 found that a snapped OSM end and its street projection can fall in neighboring DEM cells, so a 0.16 m connector on Orchard Lane read a 1.2 m step as a slope (26.5 min central one way). Fixes: every piece's slope is read over at least one DEM cell (4 m), so a shorter piece carries only its share of the height change; a snapped end within 0.5 m of the street is a level link; paths and connectors steeper than 25% are costed as stairs, as the door-to-curb rule does (15,232 m); a slope reading beyond what the line can have is capped at 35% on streets (the network builder's limit for any Berkeley street) and at twice a 32° stair on paths (316 pieces). A sanity check now runs on every build and stops it if any piece is slower than 10 s per meter with reduced mobility (twice the reduced-mobility stair climb); it also lists edges over 5 times their level walking time. After the fixes: 0 pieces flagged; 1 edge listed, a 6.3 m path at 37.901° N, 122.254° W, at 5.5 times level time and at the slope cap. Before them, 85 pieces and 204 edges were flagged (up to 349 times). Round 5 found County no-vehicle segments drawn over OSM stairways (Redwood Terrace, edge 288, on OSM way 24221421) kept in the streets-only network. Now a County no-vehicle segment named STEPS or lying on any OSM stairway or path is a path; any edge with a stair-costed piece leaves the streets-only network; and a County drivable segment of 20 m or more lying mostly on an OSM stairway is walked as that stairway: 9 segments (three on Orchard Ln, Vine Ln, Leroy Av, Bret Harte Wy, Fairlawn Dr, an alley and an unnamed segment). The County codes them as drivable, which the simulator's street network also does; whether cars can use them is not checked here and is a question for the network builder. A build check now stops if the streets-only network holds a stair-costed piece, a County no-vehicle segment on an OSM path, or a drivable segment of 20 m or more lying mostly on an OSM stairway (0, 0 and 0 in this build). Segment 2837 (Keith Av), central shelter walk on streets only: 16.4 min after round 4, 22.9 now. Audited and unchanged: door-to-curb legs (Tobler or stairs, direction from door to curb and back; DEM faults dropped), street, path and OSM pieces (Tobler on each 8 m piece in each direction), stairways and County paths over them (Fruin up or down by the sign of the rise), split connectors (short pieces, both directions), Dijkstra's reverse costs (each arc carries the time from the neighbor toward the target), scenario targets (street nodes, time 0). Without stairways and paths the medians change by at most 0.5 min and the 90th percentiles by at most 2.6 min; households on segments that are themselves stairways have no streets-only route (up to 6.4 expected carless households per scenario, 22.1 for shelters). Two rules were added after first runs gave 5-minute walks out of the upper hills: the simulator counts streets in no evacuation zone (Tilden's park roads) as safe, and with no direction rule a walker could head upwind past the fire's start. | **context**: `docs/walk-access.md`. Not applied in the simulator: walking evacuation is not modeled. |
| WA12 | **Check:** on the 40 hill stairways with step_count and a DEM rise, rise per step is a median 0.13 m (quartiles 0.07–0.18), below a typical 0.15–0.18 m riser. The 4 m DEM smooths stairs cut into slopes, so stair rises, and stair times, are probably low. | **context**: stated as a limit in the report. |
| WA13 | **Opt-in setting** (Expert view, Scenario: "Home-to-street access time: none (today's model) / from terrain", both plans; `world.walkAccess`). With it on, each departing household's departure moves later by a delay drawn from a supplementary stream of its household (`extra` salt 4 for today's households, 5 for added units, slot 0) from its segment's 10th–90th percentiles. The shared stream is never drawn for it, and the file is ignored, with a warning in the event log, if built on another network. `node tests/walk-draws.mjs`: 20 of 20 checks pass (every scenario, seeds 1–2, growth none and maximum): street, vehicles, kind, leave-early flag, ride and commuter identical, non-resident counts identical, shared stream in the same state, no departure earlier; about 10,800 of 83,017 households leave later in the north hills fire. Map: street color "Homes above or below their street (hills)", the per-segment 90th-percentile rise. | **applied**, off by default. Turning it on by default is the maintainer's decision. |
| WA14 | **Simulator comparison** (`node tools/walk-access-sim.mjs`, every scenario, medium severity, seeds 1–3, today's plan, same seed off and on): 15 pairs. People still there when embers arrived change by −80 to +251 (both directions: no clear difference); people still home at the fire front by 0 to +10; the 90% ETE by −0.4 to +0.6 min (1923 repeat 101.8–108.8 min; east wind 91.8–92.6; weekday east wind 105.2–106.4; north hills 91.6–93.4; Panoramic Hill not reached before the run ends either way). Mean delay over all departing households 6–9 s, most of them in the flats with none. At the central speeds and three legs, door-to-curb access is too short to matter against mobilization times of 2.5–3.5 hours; a reduced-mobility or more-trips setting would be larger (90th percentile 115 s) and was not run. | **context**: `docs/walk-access.md`, `data/model/walk-access-sim.json`. |
| WA15 | **Calibration and default output.** `js/sim.js` changed (`walkAccessData`, `walkDelayS`, a delay in `buildHouseholds` only when the setting is on, a log line and two summary fields). With the setting off: `node tests/headless.mjs` prints exactly what main prints (timings stripped, diffed), `node tests/plan-draws.mjs` 240 of 240 like for like, and `node tests/calibration.mjs` (3 seeds) is identical to main (diffed). Calibration did not change. | **applied**. |
